Sicut Paulus, qui ut occasionem accipiendi pseudoapostolis tolleret, stipendia praedicationis, quae secundum consuetudinem, imo secundum divinam institutionem accipere licuit, propriis manibus victum quaerens omnino accipere recusavit. De quibus parvulis adhuc subditur:

Qui defecerunt fame in capite omnium compitorum. Quid per compita, nisi humanas actiones? et quid per caput compitorum, nisi concupiscentiam carnis, quae causa est et origo omnium humanarum actionum, intelligere debemus? Parvuli ergo in capite compitorum fame deficiunt, quia carnales quique 311Dquanto magis terrenis desideriis inhiant tanto magis a pastu spiritualis alimoniae jejunant.

Consurge, lauda. MORAL. Nova quaedam vita hic mihi oriri videtur. Consurge, lauda. Quam pulcher ordo, primum transacta mala fletibus tergere, postea ad bona agenda consurgere, deinde laudare? Non est enim laus speciosa in ore peccatoris (Eccli. XV); neque potest veraciter bona agere, qui prius non studuerit efficaciter mala praeterita emendare. Lauda, inquit, in nocte in principio vigiliarum. Peccator sub flagello positus gratum Deo sacrificium immolat, si et de sua tribulatione Deum laudat. Unde recte cum dixisset propheta, lauda in nocte, hoc est in tribulatione, statim adjunxit: In principio vigiliarum. Primum etenim peccator evigilat, quando 312Amala sua agnoscere incipit. Secundo evigilat, quando mala sua agnita poenitendo corrigit. Tertio evigilat, quando ad bona agenda se convertit. Quarto evigilat, quando bona opera sua custodit. Istas quatuor vigilias in nocte praesentis vitae custodire debemus, ne videlicet nos vel in consideratione peccatorum nostrorum ignorantia fallat, vel in correctione negligentia, sive contemptus praepediat. Ne vel ad bona agenda desidia torpentes reddat, vel ad bona conservanda praesumptio minus cautos efficiat. Ille ergo in nocte in principio vigiliarum Deum laudat, qui mala, quae patitur, ex consideratione iniquitatis suae, justo Dei judicio se sustinere fatetur. Sequitur: Effunde sicut aquam cor tuum ante conspectum Domini. Caeteri liquores, cum effunduntur, 312Bvel quadam pinguedine superlinita, vel sapore infecta vasa relinquunt; sola aqua sic effunditur, ut munditia vasis nullis ejus reliquiis maculetur. Qui ergo peccata sua confitentes, aliqua vel negligentiae vel erubescentiae causa effundere timent, quasi in vase cordis spissi liquoris reliquias retinent. Qui vero cuncta quidem peccata sua per confessionem ejiciunt, sed adhuc tamen peccandi affectum non derelinquunt: ab his quidem quamvis noxius liquor prorsus ejectus sit, vasa tamen sapore infecta permanserunt. Quisquis autem per puram confessionem, et contritionem cordis sui secreta revelans, nihil aut per affectum retinet, aut per silentium tegit: ille quasi aquas cor suum ante conspectum Domini effundit. Possumus adhuc in effusione aquae 312Caliquid aedificationis attendere, quia sicut aqua effusa sordes abluit, sic confessio peccata ejiciens, conscientiam mundam reddit. Et bene ante conspectum Domini cor sicut aqua effundi dicitur, quia nisi quis cum opere pravo, etiam affectum peccandi a corde suo excludat, teste conscientia plene coram Deo non excusatur. Sequitur: Leva ad eum manus tuas. Per manus operatio significatur. Manus ergo suas poenitens ad Deum elevat, quando per exhibitionem bonorum operum coram Deo praeteritas offensas excusat. De quo adhuc subditur: Pro anima parvulorum tuorum, qui defecerunt fame in capite omnium compitorum. Per parvulos, non incongrue infirmos animae cogitatus accipimus, per caput omnium 312Dcompitorum, concupiscentiam carnis: quae omnium terrenorum desideriorum et actionum, quasi multarum viarum caput est et origo. In capite ergo compitorum parvuli nostri fame deficiunt; quia tenerae cogitationes nostrae dum desideriis carnalibus inhaerent, spiritualis alimoniae pastum perdunt. Sed nos pro anima parvulorum nostrorum manus ad Deum elevamus, cum per studium bonorum operum ad nutriendos animae cogitatus spiritualis desiderii gratiam in nobis renovari exposcimus. Qui videlicet parvuli nostri in capite compitorum fame deficiunt, quia tenerae cogitationes animi, quas intrinsecus bona desideria non pascunt, mox per carnis concupiscentiam (quae omnium malorum desideriorum caput est) emollitae, omne virtutis robur 313Aamittunt. Vel ipsa concupiscentia fames est, in qua fame deficiunt quia semper esuriunt, et expleri non possunt.

Vide, Domine, et considera quem vindemiaveris ita. LITTERAL. Non desistit a proposito iste donec coepta ad finem perducat. Hactenus enim cum peccatoribus egit, ut eos ad poenitentiam inflecteret; nunc poenitentium causam suscipiens ad Deum sermonem convertit, ut eum pro peccatoribus exoret. Simulque poenitentibus formam orandi tribuit, aperte demonstrans quantum se in precibus peccator humiliare debeat; cum ipse pro alienis peccatis orans, tam humili devotione se pietati divinae prosternat. Quia enim causam difficilem se suscepisse considerat, nequaquam aperta postulatione aures divinae 313Bmajestatis pulsare praesumit; sed cum magna reverentia, et tremore suae petitionis affectum insinuando demonstrat, dicens: Vide, Domine et considera quem videmiaveris ita. Quasi diceret: Populum tuum, quem tantum dilexisti, qui praeter te solum alium protectorem non habuit, sic vindemias, sic opprimis, sic devastas? Praesentem, quaeso, calamitatem considera, pristinum amorem ad memoriam revoca; ut si nostris precibus non flecteris, tuis saltem beneficiis ad compassionem et misericordiam movearis. Vindemiaveris, inquit. Ergo non saltus fuit populus iste, sed vinea cultorem habens, et fructum ferens. Attamen lignum vitis sine cultura et fructu inutile est, et universis lignis vilius. Cur ergo depopularis, quem colendo pretiosum, depopulando 313Cvilem, et inutilem reddis? Quem vindemiaveris, inquit, ita. Quomodo ita? tam horribiliter, tam monstruose, ut vinea ipsa devoret botros suos, et mulieres comedant filios suos. Ideo exclamat, et dicit:

Ergone, Domine, mulieres comedent fructum suum. Id est filios suos, parvulos scilicet ad mensuram palmae, id est teneros, vix dum palmae mensuram habentes; ut immanis crudelitas, imo crudelis necessitas, ostendatur. Bene autem mulieres, non matres: quia materno nomine appellandae non sunt, quae maternae pietatis memores non fuerunt. Fructum, inquit, suum comedent. Ac si diceret: Caetera germinantia ideo fructificant, ut natura generis, 313Dquae in parente consumitur, in prole germinis sui reparetur: homo vero naturae suae oblitus, fructum suum consumit, per quem reparari debuit. Sed ne sic exaggerando miseriam Deum non poscere sed provocare videatur, et quasi injustum arguere, qui tam crudeliter populum suum punierit; post commemoratam poenam, culpam etiam subjungit: callida quadam concessione utens, ut scilicet dum in culpa confitenda a justitia Dei non discrepat, in misericordia quaerenda petitionem suam citius ad effectum perducat, dicens.

Si occiditur in sanctuario Domini sacerdos, et propheta. Ac si diceret: Quia prophetas tuos, et sacerdotes tuos in sanctuario tuo occiderunt: sicut de Zacharia legitur filio Joiadae sacerdotis, quem Joas 314Arex interfecit: quia, inquam, eos occiderunt, ideo mulieres fructum uteri sui comederunt, sicut de Maria filia Eleazari legitur: et in libro Regum scriptum est (II Par. XXVI), et Josephus quoque testatur. Quod autem dicit, si occiditur, sic est ac si diceretur: Quamvis tantum peccaverint, nunquid tamen tam immani ultione plecti debuerunt? In quo tamen judicium Dei non reprehendit, sed culpam concedens, et poenam aggravans, misericordiam quaerit. Quasi diceret: Non est populus, quem tantum dilexeris: cui tanta beneficia contuleris; non est populus, qui tantum tibi peccaverit: non est populus, quem tantum dejeceris, tantum vindemiaveris atque destruxeris: et ideo, quaeso, vide, non transitorie, sed considera diligenter nostram miseriam, 314But solitam nobis iterum impendas misericordiam.

Vide, Domine, et considera quem vindemiaveris ita. ALLEG. Ecclesiam sanctam vineam appellari plurimis Scripturarum testimoniis comprobatur. De quibus est illud evangelicum, ubi paterfamilias operarios in vineam suam misisse dicitur (Matth. XX): quia videlicet Deus Pater, dum praedicatores ad erudiendam Ecclesiam dirigit, quasi ad excolendam vineam suam operarios mittit. Nam quemadmodum vitis magno quidem studio et labore excolitur, sic nimirum vita fidelium non nisi magno studio et labore ad virtutem informatur. Et quemadmodum vinea, si fecunda fuerit, quidquid sibi laboris impenditur, fructuum ubertate restaurat: sic nimirum 314Cvita fidelium, si studio disciplinae et eruditionis ad virtutem profecerit, omnem adhibitam diligentiam pretioso fructu recompensat. Item sicut lignum vitis sine fructu inutile est, et universis lignis vilius: sic profecto ii qui per fidei doctrinam excoluntur, et tamen fructum boni operis non afferunt, pejores sunt illis qui ad agnitionem veritatis nunquam pervenerunt. Et fit nonnunquam ut hos tales Deus gratiam subtrahendo, tanto profundius deserat, quanto diutius post impensam gratiam ingratos tolerabat. Quorum tamen lapsum perfecti quique ex compassione charitatis plangunt, et qui eos jam per fidem ejusdem secum corporis membra esse gaudebant praecisionem eorum sine dolore ferre non possunt. 314DEx quorum voce hic dicitur: Vide, Domine, et considera quem vindemiaveris ita. Malos Deus vindemiare dicitur, cum eos, quos ipse prius per praedicatores suos excoluit, subtracta gratia, steriles et inanes derelinquit. Potest etiam vindemiatio ipsa ad electos referri: quia nimirum cum quidam per infidelitatem ab unitate Ecclesiae subtrahuntur, ipsa sancta Ecclesia nuda sibi, et quasi spoliata remansisse videtur. Unde cum magno pietatis affectu pronuntiandum est hoc, quod dicitur. Vide, Domine, et considera quem videlicet populum tuum, pusillum gregem tuum, vindemiaveris ita. Quomodo? Subjungit: Ergone comedent mulieres fructum suum, parvulos ad mensuram palmae? Per mulieres recte malos praelatos accipimus sensu carnali et fluxis 315Amoribus emollitos: qui fructum suum comedunt, quia de labore ministerii sui in praesenti vita remunerationem accipiunt. Econtrario bonis doctoribus dictum est: Posui vos ut eatis, et fructum afferatis, et fructus vester maneat (Joan. XV). Electorum namque fructus permanere dicitur: quia in eis pro labore non transitoria merces, sed praemium aeternum conservatur. Mulieres ergo comedunt fructum suum quia carnales quique dum in hac vita laboris sui mercedem accipiunt, quasi fructum non conservant, sed consumunt. Quis sit autem fructus iste, declaratur, cum dicitur: Parvulos ad mensuram palmae. Parvulos hic non aetate, sed fide intelligere debemus. Per palmam vero initium boni operis non inconvenienter accipimus. Quid est ergo quod fructus 315Bmulierum parvuli dicuntur, nisi quod fides credentium doctoribus ad praemium reputatur? Quos videlicet carnales praelati comedunt: quia nimirum dum terrenis inhiant, infirmos fide et incipientes per exemplum mali operis ad interitum pertrahunt, quos prius per verbum praedicationis in fide genuerunt. Vel certe tunc mulieres parvulos suos comedunt: quando mali praelati in Ecclesia subjectos quosque, quos cibo coelestis alimoniae pascere debuerant, rebus suis amare spoliando per inopiam affligunt. Sequitur: Si occiditur in sanctuario Domini sacerdos, et propheta? Per sacerdotem, et prophetam, eosdem hic intelligere debemus quos paulo ante per mulieres significatos accepimus: praelatos scilicet qui et per verbum prophetae sunt, et per ministerium sacerdotes. 315CPer sanctuarium autem cultus divinus exprimitur: ac si diceretur: Si praelati spiritualia indigne administrando pereunt: quare ergo scelera eorum subjectos innocentes involvunt?

Vide, Domine, et considera quem vindemiaveris ita. MORAL. Congrua similitudine anima a Deo vindemiari dicitur, quando donis virtutum malis meritis suis exigentibus justo Dei judicio spoliatur. Sed est quaedam vindemiatio caeteris perniciosior. Minus namque periculosum est virtutem perdere, quam de virtute superbire. Qui enim virtutem perdit, saltem detrimentum suum agnoscit: qui autem de virtute superbit, damnum tolerat, ignorat. Propheta plangens animam, non solum peccatricem, sed et de virtute falsa inaniter gloriantem - Vide, inquit, Domine, 315Det considera, quem vindemiaveris ita. Et quasi quaerens quomodo ita? exclamando subjungit, et dicit: Ergone comedent mulieres fructum suum? Mulieres namque fructum suum comedunt, quando carnales animae de virtutis opere se per inanem gloriam pascunt. Fructum comedunt, quia inde virtus ipsa consumitur, unde mens illicita refectione delectatur. Fructum, inquit, suum. Qualem fructum? Parvulos ad mensuram palmae. Per parvulos, accipere possumus virtutes teneras, et nondum ad robur virile promotas: per palmam vero, boni operis initium. Parvuli ergo ad mensuram palmae sunt virtutes tenerae, et ad initium bonae operationis noviter productae. Quid est ergo quod mulieres parvulos ad 316Amensuram palmae comedere dicuntur nisi quod mentes carnales etiam de initio boni operis gloriantur? Sequitur: Si occiditur in sanctuario Domini sacerdos, et propheta? Mens justi, quae sanctuarium Dei est, duo in se habere debet: prudentiam scilicet et devotionem: prudentiam, per quam instantia mala prospiciendo calcet; devotionem, per quam mala praeterita supplicando abstergat. Quid ergo devotionem, nisi quemdam animae sacerdotem expiationis sacrificium jugiter in conspectu Domini offerentem; et quid prudentiam, nisi prophetam accipimus? Quia ergo sacerdos, et propheta, in sanctuario Domini occiditur, mulieres parvulos suos comedunt: quia nunquam mens hominis de se virtute sua extolleret, nisi prius per negligentiam, et praeteritotorum 316Bmalorum memoria, et futurorum cautela in ea tepuisset.

Jacuerunt in terra furoris puer, et senes [senex]: virgines meae, et juvenes mei in gladio ceciderunt. LITTERAL. Vel sic distinguendum est: Puer et senex in terra jacuerunt, virgines et juvenes in gladio ceciderunt. Sive ita: Puer, et senex, et virgines in terra jacuerunt, et juvenes in gladio ceciderunt. Quod propterea fortassis magis congruit: quia virgines in bello gladio perimi non solent, sed in direptionem, et depraedationem adduci, nisi ex hoc ipso atrocitas hostium designetur, qui nec virginibus parcerent. Dicit ergo populum bellatorem cum regibus et principibus gladio corruisse, reliquam multitudinem diversi sexus et aetatis in captivitate distractam. 316CPuer, inquit, et senex, et virgines: infirma aetas, infirmus sexus, in terra jacuerunt, sine misericordia, sine humanitate abjecti, in terra furoris. Non solum in terra, quod ad poenam sufficere posset ejusmodi, sed in terra furoris. Vel in terra sua, quam Dominus in furore suo vastari, et depopulari permisit: vel in terra aliena, ad quam furor Domini eos expulit. Sequitur:

Interfecisti in die furoris, percussisti, nec misereris [misertus es ]. Quasi dicat: Tibi, Domine, nostram calamitatem ascribimus: de nostra miseria tecum cansam inimus. Quia plagam, quam hostis saevientis furor intulit, ira tua nostris prius peccatis provocata, dictavit. Interfecisti eos, qui in gladio ceciderunt: percussisti eos, qui in terra jacuerunt: nec 316Dmisereris, ut cum tot jam periisse videas, ab iis saltem, qui superstites adhuc sunt, flagellum tuae indignationis avertas. Quaeso, Domine, obsecro, Domine, miserere: ut exitium, quod te irascente incidimus, te miserante, evadamus.

Jacuerunt in terra puer, et senes. ALLEG. Sicut populus, sic et sacerdotes. In terra jacuerunt, in terrenis desideriis quieverunt. Nec solum quieverunt (quod superius significatum est, cum dictum est: sederunt in terra, conticuerunt senes), sed et dormierunt: quia jacuerunt, ut jam se ipsos nesciant: et omni ratione sopita unde venerint, non considerent, aut quo tendant. Quibus per Paulum dicitur: Surge, qui dormis: et exsurge a mortuis, et 317Ailluminabi te Christus (Ephes. V). Heu! quam male jacent, qui in terra jacent! quam male dormiunt, qui in terra dormiunt! qui in terra requiescunt, et cum evigilant, nihil divitiarum in manibus suis inveniunt! Quidquid enim temporaliter amatur, quasi somnium est, et evanescit, cum homo in morte evigilare coeperit. In terra, inquit, furoris jacuerunt. Quid per terram furoris, nisi hunc mundum accipere debemus: in quo genus humanum propter primi reatus vindictam, de paradisi felicitate ejectum, et exsilio damnatum est? Quanta ergo vesania, ut in hac peregrinatione homo requiem quaerat, quo se in ira divinae ultionis projectum esse non dubitat? Sequitur: Virgines meae, et juvenes mei in gladio ceciderunt. Per gladium 317Bin hoc loco doctrina haereticorum signatur, de quibus in psalmo dicitur: Lingua eorum gladius aculus (Psal. LVI); quia dum aliquos ab unitate catholicae fidei ad suos errores pertrahunt, quasi membra a corpore praecidunt. Per virgines autem et juvenes, fideles signantur, qui et virgines sunt, quia integritatem fidei pravis operibus corrumpunt; et juvenes sunt, quia per constantiam mentis adversis ingruentibus non cedunt. Quid est ergo quod virgines in gladio cadere dicuntur, nisi quia, testante Apostolo, bonos mores mala colloquia corrumpunt (I Cor. XV), et dogmata haereticorum, in quibus fidei castitatem violare praevalent, postmodum etiam bonorum operum integritatem subvertunt? Juvenes in gladio cadunt, cum ii quorum constantia adversis 317Csuperari non potuit, dolis verborum succisi ad infidelitatem corruunt. Vel si alteram distinctionem sequimur, virgines in terra jacent, quando ii mente terrenis desideriis inhiant, qui foris per exhibitionem operis castitatem simulant intentionis. Per juvenes etiam, superbos, et de suis viribus praesumentes intelligere possumus, quia vita homini in hac praecipue aetate et calore et robore viget. Juvenes ergo in gladio cadunt, quia cum, Deus, superbos despicias, ii qui haereticorum erroribus subvertuntur, constat quod nequaquam a cognitione veritatis caderent, nisi prius alta de se sapuissent. Sequitur: Interfecisti in die furoris; percussisti, nec misereris. Interfecit eos, qui per infidelitatem corruerunt; 317Dpercussit eos, qui in lapsu pereuntium per passionem charitatis vulnerati sunt. Non miseretur, quia dum lapsos non erigit, etiam dolorem stantium non consolatur.

Jacuerunt in terra furoris puer, et senes. MORAL. Terra furoris est caro nostra mortalis, quae ante peccatum primi hominis virtutum germina protulit; postea autem ex maledictionis sententia spinas et tribulos vitiorum germinare coepit Parvulus autem est sensus carnis, quia, quantum in se est, semper puerilia quaedam et vana appetit. Sicut autem per parvulum sensum carnis accipimus, ita per senes judicium et appetitum mentis intelligere non inconvenienter possumus; quia et in discernendo mens esse debet matura, et in appetendo non temeraria. 318AParvulus ergo cum senibus in terra furoris jacet, quando id etiam nostrae corruptionis, quod vindicta praevaricationis intulit; carnalis mens non solum secundum sensum, sed etiam secundum judicium, et appetitum sibi ad delectationem substernit, et ibi se putat requiescere, ubi projecta est vapulare. Sequitur: Virgines meae, et juvenes mei in gladio ceciderunt. Per virgines, castas cogitationes; per juvenes, fortitudinem atque constantiam animi; per gladium, tentationem adversarii accipere non inconvenienter possumus. Quia enim aetas juventutis ampliori robore viget, quid aliud fortitudo bene agendi et perseverandi constantia, quam quidam juvenes sunt in animo rationali? quia et ad inchoandum opus prompti sunt, et in coepto opere sine defectu 318Bpersistunt. Recte ergo prius senes in terra jacuisse dicuntur, ac deinde virgines et juvenes in gladio cecidisse memorantur; quia ex quo judicium mentis et appetitus terrena delectatione corrumpitur, superveniens tentatio facile et bonarum cogitationum integritatem violat, et fortitudinem atque constantiam animi subnervat. Quia enim, ut saepe dictum est, cogitationes ab affectibus prodeunt, dum ex corruptis cogitationes corrumpuntur, quasi ex infecta radice rami amaritudinem trahunt. Cumque carnalis animus extra carnem nihil appetit aut discernit, omnis mox fortitudo ejus atque constantia, in tentationem carnis emollescit. Neque hoc praetereundum est quod cum senes in terra sedent, virgines capita sua cinere spargunt; cum senes cilicio 318Cinduuntur, virgines capita in terram abjiciunt, cum senes in terra jacent, virgines in gladio cadunt; quia profecto secundum corruptionem affectuum crescit corruptio cogitationum. Sequitur: Interfecisti in die furoris, percussisti, nec misereris. Quasdam virtutes Deus aliquando in animo per tentationem perimi, quasdam vulnerari permittit, ut dum tentatus aliqua adhuc bona se retinuisse videt, prorsus in desperationem non corruat; dum vero quaedam se perdidisse aspicit, de suis viribus non praesumat; et tanto verius id, quod amisit, recuperet, quanto subtilius suo damno edoctus, idipsum, quod retinuit, ex se non esse jam videt. Sed quia multi non ad correptionem, sed ad subversionem 318Dtentari permittuntur, idcirco homo, qui suae tentationis causam semper scire non potest, periculum semper timere debet, propter quod recte ex voce trepidantium subjungitur: Nec misereris. Quae nimirum sententia timoris est, non assertionis. Quia enim cito a tentatione non liberat, ideo tentatus flagellum suum irae, et non misericordiae esse putat; quod tamen desperantis non est, sed timentis, ut diximus.

Vocasti quasi ad diem solemnem. LITTERAL. Ac si diceret: Inimici quidem semper ad nocendum parati sunt; sed tamen, nisi vocentur, venire non possunt. Non igitur arguit, sed honorat Deum iste, qui nihil viribus inimicorum tribuit, sed divinam potentiam etiam in sui oppressione extollit. Vocasti, 319Ainquit. Quasi diceret: Eorum fuit velle, tuum concedere, ut ex hoc ipso attendas, quod et tuum est liberare. Vocasti quasi ad diem solemnem, id est ad diem laetitiae et gaudii: non quod bonitas tua in nostra miseria delectata sit, sed quia malitia eorum in hoc exsultavit, ut idipsum te ad miserendum provocet, et citius nobis compatiaris, dum eos inique super nos gaudere conspicis.

Qui terrerent me de circuitu. Qui me undique arcerent, ne manus persequentium evaderent, ad similitudinem venatorum qui clamore feras in retia compellunt. Notat tempus illud quando Judaeis fugientibus Babylonios finitimae nationes circumquaque eis fugae praesidium obstruxerunt, ne evaderent manus inimicorum quando Sedechias cum exercitu 319Bbellatorum fugiens in campo Hiericonteo comprehensus est, et reductus in Reblata ad regem Babylonis, ibique filiis ejus coram eo interfectis, ipse caecatus est, et abductus in Babylonem. Unde sequitur:

Et non fuit in die furoris Domini qui effugeret, et relinqueretur. Relinqueretur, subauditur, vivus; vel relinqueretur in terra sua, et non abduceretur captivus.

Quos educavi, et nutrivi. Educavi ad disciplinam, et nutrivi ad fortitudinem: qui magis plangendi.

Inimicus consumpsit eos. Id est, usque ad internecionem delevit.

Vocasti quasi ad diem solemnem: qui terrerent me de circuitu. ALLEG. Potest non inconvenienter 319Cin hoc loco tempus extremi examinis designari: quae dies vel ideo solemnis dicitur, quia tunc gaudia justorum inchoabunt, sive quia tunc omnia in medium discutienda deducentur. Quod nimirum tempus ita nemo sine terrore exspectat, sicut nemo est, cui sine culpa praesentis vitae cursum transire contingat. Electi vero dum se pro praesentis vitae miseria lamentis afficiunt, etiam mala quae restant ante oculos mentis adducunt, et sollicite futuros terrores considerando, ut ibi judicium evadant, hic se judicant. Considerant saevissimos exactores daemones, ad quorum praesentiam etiam electi terrebuntur: qui undique arctabunt judicandos, ita ut nemo locum evadendi habeat, nisi misericordia judicis 319Ddistrictionem temperaret ultionis. Quando multos, qui hic in disciplina Ecclesiae educati, et doctrina veritatis enutriti inter filios Dei computandi videbantur, inimicus consumet et secum ad perditionem trahet. Potest etiam de paganis et haereticis, sive quibuslibet infidelibus dici, praecipue in tempore Antichristi, quando diabolus undique in membris suis ad persecutionem fidelium laxabitur. Qui dies ideo solemnis dicitur, quia tunc iniqui de oppressione bonorum laetabuntur; quia non est qui effugiat et relinquatur, dum alios in veritate persistentes corporaliter puniunt, alios autem sibi consentientes spiritualiter occidunt. Unde sequitur: Quos educavi, et nutrivi, inimicus consumpsit; quia nimirum multi tunc ad infidelitatem corruent, 320Aqui in disciplina Ecclesiae educati, et doctrina enutriti fuerunt. Educavi, inquit, et enutrivi, non solum nutrivi, quia fortes etiam, quos pondus tribulationis oppressit, inimicus consumpsit. Bonos quidem persequendo affligere inimicus potest, consumere autem non potest; qui autem tribulatione superatur, ille ab inimico consumitur.

Vocasti quasi ad diem solemnem, qui terrerent me de circuitu. Vita humana quasi circuitus quidam est, quae post excursum actionum temporalium, illuc tandem redit per mortem, unde prodiit per nativitatem. Sed in hoc circuitu fidelis anima diem solemnem celebrat; quia, dum per internum gaudium in amore aeternorum figitur, ab omni foris illicita occupatione feriatur. Cum vero animus anteacta 320Bmala ad memoriam revocat, et ex eorum consideratione amplius per compunctionem accenditur, quasi de circuitu celebraturi ad solemnitatem invitantur. Sicut per Psalmistam dicitur: Reliquiae cogitationum diem festum agent tibi (Psal. LXXV). Sed quia peccatoris mentem ante compunctionis ardorem praeteritorum malorum recordatio quodam sui reatus terrore concutit, recte istos de circuitu, et ad solemnitatem pariter, et ad terrorem vocatos dicit. Sciendum tamen est quod aliter ille terretur qui nihil boni egisse se meminit, atque aliter ille qui bonis actibus suis quaedam, pro quibus timeat, mala admista cognoscit. Quando igitur homo circumspicit actiones suas, et in eis quaedam invenit ubi confidat, quaedam vero pro quibus timeat: terretur 320Cquidem, sed non de circuitu, quia in altare peccatrix conscientia ex memoria sui reatus stringitur; ex altera parte in fiducia boni operis dilatatur. Sed dum peccatoris animum ex omni parte facta sua accusant, tunc quasi in circuitu terretur; quia hinc inde omnia formidanda conspiciens, per solam angustiam intra semetipsum coarctatur. Hoc etiam convenienter in persona peccatricis animae dicitur quando de corpore exiens undique malorum suorum memoria occurrente turbatur. Unde et recte dictum est: Vocasti. Homo namque quandiu in hac vita est, debet ante acta mala assidue ad memoriam revocare, ut se ipse spontaneo terreat ad poenitentiam, sicut Psalmista de se testatur dicens: 320DPeccatum meum coram [contra] me est semper (Psal. L). Et in alio loco idem ait: Cogitabo pro peccato meo (Psal. XXXVII). Quisquis autem hic malorum suorum recordari noluit, illic ea Deus in testimonium damnationis ante oculos mentis ejus vocabit, quatenus ex memoria eorum ibi terreatur ad poenam, qui hic salubriter noluit terreri ad poenitentiam. Sicut divina vox per eumdem Psalmistam loquitur, dicens: Arguam te, et statuam contra faciem tuam (Psal. XLIX). Cujus vocationis modus adhuc demonstratur cum dicitur: Quasi ad diem solemnem. Dies enim solemnis, est dies quietis; dies vocationis quando operari non licet, sed iis quisque tunc cum gaudio frui incipit, quae prius cum labore sibi praeparavit. Quid ergo est dies mortis, nisi 321Adies solemnis, in qua jam amplius non restat facultas operandi; sed quisque operum suorum mercede et praemio incipit praeteritorum laborum perfrui? Sequitur:

Et non fuit in die furoris Domini qui effugeret, et relinqueretur. Dies furoris Domini, finis uniuscujusque est: quando quisque cum magna districtione ad reddendam rationem compellitur: qui prius in vita sua quasi in die misericordiae benigne ad poenitentiam exspectabatur. Recte ergo de die furoris dicitur: Non fuit qui effugeret, et relinqueretur. Effugeret, scilicet conscientiam: relinqueretur ad poenitentiam. Notate, quod dicitur: Effugeret, scilicet conscientiam. Magnum tormentum est conscientia mala. Sed peccatores hic conscientiam 321Bsuam effugere possunt: dum exterioribus delectationibus dediti, mala, quae intrinsecus tolerant, interim quodammodo oblivioni tradunt. Minus enim nunc spina conscientiae cor pungit: quando eam (ut ita dicam) terrenae delectationis fascia obvolutam contegit. Ibi autem conscientiae tormentum effugere non potuerunt: quia cum sensus carnis in morte foris clauditur, horror praeteritorum malorum intus animo aperitur. Tunc infelix anima licet sero compuncta vellet jam per poenitentiam emendare, quod non potest per conscientiam effugere. Sed justo Dei judicio, quae prius tempora poenitenti indulta sibi neglexit, jam non relinquitur, quia semel irrevocabili sententia emissa, amplius in hac vita ad poenitentiam non differtur. Recte ergo dicitur. 322ANon fuit in die furoris Domini qui effugeret, et relinqueretur. Quia cum mortis hora supervenerit, tunc quisque ad conscientiam suam redire compellitur, ut ipsa comitante ad excipiendam sententiam judicis sine dilatione ab hac vita transferatur. Sequitur: Quos educavi, et enutrivi, inimicus consumpsit eos. Ne solam conscientiae poenam peccatoribus praeparari putes: quos educavi, inquit, et enutrivi, inimicus consumpsit eos: quia nimirum virtus, et pulchritudo mortalium corporum, quae hic per illicita desideria pascitur, illic a tortoribus angelis in tormentis consumetur. Quae ideo consumi dicitur, non quod substantiam poena in nihilum redigat, sed quia dolor usque ad intima naturae percurrens, nihil intactum relinquat. Vel per educatos, 322Bet enutritos, ipsa carni desideria accipere possumus: quae educantur per superbiam, et enutriuntur per luxuriam: et quanto magis ea explendo pascimus, tanto magis ad appetitum inflammamus. Hos educatos, et enutritos consumit inimicus, id est, mors de qua dicit Apostolus: Novissime inimica destruetur mors (I Cor. XV): quia nimirum dum caro nostra in morte succiditur, omnia ejus desideria pariter exstinguuntur, sicut per Psalmistam dicitur: In illa die peribunt omnes cogitationes eorum (Psal. XLV). Et considera quam convenienter in fine lamenti novissima poena peccatoris animae plangitur: ut intelligamus ea mala, quae praedicta sunt, quanta miseria consequatur.

Little Notes on Lamentations

255D(THREN. I.) How doth the city sit alone, that was full of people? So far as concerns the letter, Jeremiah bewails the desolation of Jerusalem, and this is the voice of one wondering or grieving. And he begins from wonder for this reason, that he may show the greatness of the calamity, and by this make his hearers attentive: that they may recognize in what evils they are, and be turned to repentance. How doth the city sit alone, that was full of people? as if he should say: 256DThe city which once, in the time of David and of the other good kings who pleased God, was full of people — mark why it has now remained alone. For why, unless because they offended God? But he says alone, that is, desolate, because of the people led away captive into Babylon. Or, if it be referred to the same time, she is full of people, and yet sits alone; because she has not God propitious, since 257Aa multitude of people cannot profit where the help of God is wanting. But that he says, Sitteth, pertains to casting down and to humiliation.

She is become as a widow, that is, forsaken while her husband is yet living: and therefore not a widow, but as a widow; because if she shall have repented, she will still be able to be reconciled. And God is called the husband of that people for this reason; because he had joined her to himself in chaste love for his own worship, that she might not commit fornication through the various cults of idols.

The mistress of the nations is become as a widow; the prince of the provinces is become under tribute. So it was of old, that the peoples of foreigners served the Jews, and the provinces of the nations were subject to them; but now they themselves, forsaken by God, are made tributary to the nations. 257BThe recollection therefore of the former glory is the heightening of the present misery.

According to the sense of allegory, Jeremiah signifies in the Church any spiritual men whatever: who, when they see that a multitude of men has flowed together to the faith, and that the name of Christ has been spread through almost the whole world, but find none, or exceedingly few, who follow Christ in truth and sincerely hold his faith — all men seeking the things that are their own — grieving and groaning say: How doth the city sit alone, that was full of people? Why do we discern so great a people in the Church, and yet see the Church to be alone? because we can scarcely find anyone who is truly with the Church. I have found something similar in the Gospel, the Lord being set in the crowd, 257Cthe people hemming him in on every side and pressing upon him: There came a woman with an issue of blood; and coming behind she touched the hem of his garment: and he straightway: Who, he saith, hath touched me? (Marc. V.) Did someone touch me? For he who, when the woman touched the hem, asks as though he had suffered something new, who touched me, plainly declares that before (although all were jostling and pressing) he had not been touched. As therefore Christ remains untouched while the crowd presses, so the Church, the body of Christ, sits alone among many; because the catholic faith has many professors, few imitators, just as then too those who were near the Lord by bodily presence could not touch him; because they were far off by faith and dilelectionem. 257DThe spiritual Jeremiah therefore laments, and says: How doth the city sit alone, that was full of people? For wherever the servants of God are, they cannot see these things without grief and groaning. The mistress of the nations is become as a widow; the prince of the provinces is become under tribute. By the nations we rightly understand all carnal men placed within the Church; but by the provinces, all whosoever are established outside the Church, such as are pagans, Jews and heretics. Then therefore is holy Church mistress of the nations, when by the rigor of discipline she subjects all the carnal placed within to serve her: she is prince of the provinces, when by power she repels the unbelievers placed without, that they may not be able to harm. But if perchance, when for the demands of sins she is forsaken by God, so that she is able neither to restrain those who trouble her within, 258Anor to defend herself from those who assail her without: then the mistress of the nations is left as a widow, and the prince of the provinces is made tributary. For when she beholds the faithful drawn away to unbelief, whether deceived by heretics, or broken by the powers of this world through reward or through terror, what else does she do than pay tribute to foreigners?

According to the moral understanding, the city signifies the soul, which sits alone when it is forsaken by God; but full of a people of virtues, when it is inhabited by God. But if we have found the city whose desolation is lamented, where is Jeremiah to be found? Each one of us ought to be a Jeremiah, and to lament his own desolation just as he lamented the desolation of Jerusalem. And surely, if he so lamented 258Bthe ruin of stones, we much more ought to lament the desolation of our souls, and each one to say: How doth the city sit alone, that was full of people? How is my soul made desolate? How has it lost that good inhabitant, in whose presence it was once full of a people of virtues? The mistress of the nations is become as a widow. The nations are the desires of the flesh, which are inborn in us according to the corruption of our first nativity, and gainsay the law of the mind: over which the soul then rules well, when it is perfectly made subject to God. The prince of the provinces is become under tribute. By the provinces we can understand the bodily senses, because, as in one province there are many men, so each of the senses has diverse motions and diverse operations, through which it is poured out abroad 258Cupon visible things; and while the appetites of the several senses are moved at the beck of reason, the soul holds rule as over certain provinces. If therefore the soul desires to rule over what is beneath it, that is, over sensuality, it is necessary that it make itself subject to what is above it, that is, to God; because it will in no wise be able to be troubled beneath itself by what is beneath it, so long as it shall have God above it as its governor. But if, forgetful of the fear of the Lord, it has followed its own concupiscences, God takes away his grace from it, and then of necessity it succumbs nervelessly to those desires which before, when it was subject to God and was ruled by him, it powerfully constrained — the appetite of its own senses — to the command of reason.

And note that he says mistress, not prince, of the nations, 258Dand prince, not mistress, of the provinces; because vices, which are not natural, ought to be repressed; for the senses, because they are natural, must not be repressed but ruled, so that in extirpating the former a man should study to be strict, in moderating and guarding the latter, discreet. But it happens sometimes that, while a man neglects to resist the lawful desires of his flesh and to guard the motions of his senses, he is at length so bound by depraved custom that afterwards, even willing it, he cannot resist those same desires. When therefore he is compelled to serve the vices to which before he consented of his own accord, what else does he do than pay tribute to depraved custom? Three goods therefore and three evils has he enumerated. The goods are: a city full of people, mistress of the nations, prince of the provinces; the evils: alone, a widow, tributary. But let us see 259Afirst how she obtains the goods, afterwards how she descends to the evils. Before the Holy Spirit comes to our heart, our soul is barren; but when he has come, he makes it fruitful, that it may bring forth and that virtues may be born in it. Which virtues, so long as they are still imperfect and beginning, and cannot yet go forth abroad, but are still nourished within by the grace of the same Spirit, that they may grow and become sturdy, what else are they than certain little ones brought up in the father's house, until they come to lawful age? But when they have come to full increase, and have begun to use a certain solid food of wisdom, then they no longer need to be nourished as little ones, but ought to live as a certain people in a city under the law of its own ruler. But when God, 259Bpresiding within, rules us, then the flesh, made subject, serves without; and the more humbly we are made subject to him within, the more sturdily we hold rule without. If therefore our soul was within full of a people of virtues, when it had God as king; it was also without the mistress of the nations, that is, of carnal desires, and prince of the provinces, that is, of the senses of the body. But now alone, because it has lost its king; a widow, because it has lost its husband; tributary, because, made subject to vices, it serves.

Weeping she hath wept in the night. The driving home of the word signifies the abundance of grief. Weeping she hath wept in the night, that is, in the time of rest, in the time of forgetfulness, when men are wont to forget their own evils. And mark how great is the misery of her from whom even then 259Cgriefs cannot be absent, when others rest from griefs.

And her tears are on her cheeks. There is sometimes a grief which touches the heart in some fashion, but does not suffice to wring out tears. Not such is the grief of this woman; for her tears are on her cheeks.

There is none to comfort her among all her dear ones. Either because they despise her, set as she is in tribulation, or because in so great an evil they cannot come to her aid. And he calls the dear ones of that people the prophets and the princes, who had been wont to comfort them: the prophets, by promising help from God; and the princes, by purging away enemies and defending the fatherland. But now they in no wise comforted the people set in tribulation, because the one sort preached God angry, and the other could not resist the enemies 259Dlaying waste the country and taking the people captive. Or it can be read otherwise: weeping she hath wept in the night, that is, in secret, in hiding: which is a thing loved [amicum] by those who weep, who will not be comforted, but are fed upon their own griefs. Or the people set in captivity weeps in hiding for this reason, that it does not dare to make its sadness manifest, on account of the cruel lords to whom it is subject, lest it should stir up their greater wrath against itself, if it appeared sorrowful over its own servitude. But so great a grief cannot be hidden, because her tears are on her cheeks, that is, in the open, in the manifest, because from the constancy of weeping the face has swollen. And in these so great evils, what comfort could they look for, 260Ato whom even their dearest bring no comfort!

All her friends have despised her. By friends he would have understood the neighboring nations once joined by treaty to the people of the Jews, who now have not only despised that people, set as it is in distresses, by bringing it no help, but have even become its enemies by persecuting it. According to the sense of allegory the night is sinners, but the cheeks are the preachers who by expounding it break small the food of the word of God, and so pass it on to the weak and to those dull of understanding. But when the Church, in this exile of the present life, weeps in her more perfect members for the falls of those who grow weak, she bears, like a people set in captivity, tears on her cheeks through the night. Or the night can signify this very present life in which we live, 260Bwhen as yet we do not see one another's consciences. And, as we said above, the cheeks signify those who have the knowledge of the word of God. Then therefore does holy Church bear tears on her cheeks through the night, when all the perfect, the more nearly they behold by the illumination of the mind the day of eternity, so much the more lament the darkness of the present life, according to the sentence of Solomon, in which he says: He that addeth knowledge, addeth sorrow [laborem] There is none to comfort her among all her dear ones (Eccles. I). By the dear ones of the Church we understand those same sinners whom above we said were signified by the night; whom assuredly, while she laments them, she loves, because she would in no wise lament over their perdition, unless she loved their salvation and their conversion. But then she receives no comfort from 260Cthem, when none of them returns to repentance, because the comfort of those who weep would be the conversion of sinners. Or the dear ones of the Church are those blessed angelic spirits, or the souls of the saints, to whose fellowship she sighs out of the night of this exile; who then bring her no comfort, while they still suffer her to be a pilgrim away from their company. Of whom it is further added. All her friends have despised her. Whom does he here call friends of the Church, save the powerful of this world, who sometimes, while they see her exalted in temporal things, honor her and feign to love her, but when they behold her set in distresses, persecute her and are despised? According to the moral sense the soul has its own day, has its own night. It has day, when it is raised up into the light of contemplation; 260Dit has night when it is darkened by the gloom of temptations. But in the day it laughs, in the night it weeps, because the mind which groans, pressed by the weight of temptations, afterwards, being lifted up, grows glad in the joy of contemplation. The soul therefore weeps in the night, when it pricks its darkened conscience within with wholesome grief. It bears tears on its cheeks, when by strict chastisement without it afflicts the flesh. For then are the tears on the cheeks, when the griefs of the heart reach even to the maceration of the flesh. Whereupon, for a yet greater heightening of the grief, there is subjoined:

There is none to comfort her among all her dear ones. In three ways is a man forsaken by God: sometimes 261Awithin and not without, sometimes without and not within, sometimes both without and within. Job was forsaken without and not within, who outwardly endured the scourges of the flesh, but within did not lose constancy of mind. David was forsaken within and not without, whose mind lust subjected to itself within by consent, but the prophet's admonition without called him back to repentance. That prodigal son in the Gospel was forsaken within and without, who both, living luxuriously, ran to waste within, and, wasting away with hunger, found no comfort without. But of those whom God deserts in this manner, some he deserts for their testing, that they may be exercised by temptation; others he deserts for their overthrow, that they may be cast down by temptation. For which cause the Psalmist also prays: Turn not aside in wrath from thy servant (Psal. XXVI); as if 261Bhe should say: Even if thou permittest me to be tempted, do not let me be led into temptation, that is, to be overcome by temptation. But because a man cannot penetrate the depth of the divine judgments, then most of all does each one set in temptation dread that he has been forsaken, when he beholds himself harried by temptations both within and without. But he bears adversities without more easily whose conscience within the delight of sin does not trouble. And again, he endures temptation within more easily who has comfort without. Whence also the great tribulation of this woman is shown, who is demonstrated to have been forsaken both within and without; for this which is said: Weeping she hath wept in the night, and her tears are on her cheeks, intimates that she was forsaken within; but that which is said: There is none to comfort her among all 261Cher dear ones, declares that she was desolate without. There follows: All her friends have despised her, and are become her enemies. See how many are the tribulations of the just. Perhaps it was too little for her, set in distresses, that she received no comfort from her dear ones, unless she also endured persecution from her friends. But who are these friends who persecute us, save those of whom it shall be said in the Gospel: A man's enemies are they of his own household? (Matth. X.) These therefore are our enemies, our persecutors, our own household, our friends, near to us according to the affinity of the flesh, who love us for the present life by a love of the flesh, but gainsay us as we walk in the way of God. For when we desire to remove ourselves far from the love of this world, 261Dstraightway we find those who before seemed friends to be adversaries. First indeed, when we wish to be converted to God, they strive under the pretext of a pestilent affection to call us back with flattering persuasions. But if they see them fixed and immovable in their good purpose, presently they disown and spurn them as adversaries, and sometimes even, by hounding them with most savage hatreds and afflicting them with punishments, from false friends they become true persecutors. Let him say therefore, there is none to comfort her among all her dear ones, because in tribulation she does not deserve to receive the words of the just for her comfort; all her friends have despised her, and are become her enemies, because from the wicked she suffers insult and contempt.

Judah hath departed. He passed over, he withdrew, he fled from his own land into Babylon, because he could not endure the great 262Aservitude with which he was afflicted by the nations, judging that it would be more tolerable for him if he served one nation in a foreign land than if he were a prey to all the nations in his own.

He dwelt among the nations, nor hath he found rest. It is proper to the afflicted that they always judge the present peril the heavier. As the sick in the night look for the day, and in the day desire the night, and while they always long for their pain to pass, always tend toward pain, so surely this people, while it was afflicted in its own land, sought flight; but when it was made desolate in exile, and, wandering as a vagabond, could not find rest, it sighed for return. And here he expresses the feeling of those who waver, not because they departed of their own accord, but because, set in straitness, they changed their wishes to a contrary 262Bmind.

All her persecutors have taken her in the midst of straits. Hemmed in on every side, he could find no place of escape; fleeing the Chaldeans, he fell among the Egyptians; and when he was fleeing from the Egyptians, he ran into the Assyrians.

Mystically, Judah, who is interpreted confessing, signifies certain men in the Church who confess the name of Christ; but because they are not yet firm in the love of Christ they blush to suffer adversities for Christ. Of such it is said: They believe for a time, and in the time of temptation they fall away (Luc. VIII). These therefore, on account of the affliction and the multitude of servitude, in which, while they will not share in the sufferings of the just, become strangers to the fellowship of the just. He dwelt among the nations. 262CTo dwell among the nations is to imitate the life and conversation of the depraved. Not to find rest is to be entangled in the actions of this world; for because in the actions of this world there is no end, there can be no rest for those who pursue them. Yet often a man for love of the present life willingly bears the labors which for the love of God he was refusing to bear. There is made therefore a great reproach against those who depart from God, and at the same time excuse is taken away from them, since it is openly shown that they transgressed without cause, when by declining useful labors they came down to useless labors. All her persecutors have taken her in the midst of straits. So long as the heart of a man is enlarged by charity and by the hope of eternal goods, if perchance it endures without in tribulation, yet within it has no straitness. How great a good therefore 262Dhe loses who loses the confidence which is in God, a man can recognize from this, that the heart always suffers straitness in adversities, unless it be enlarged by the hope of future goods. In the midst of straits, he says, they have seized her. And consider what are the straits of those who depart from God: they are never secure, they always tremble; in prosperity they fear, in adversity they despair.

Judah hath departed. There are some who, while they look upon their own sins, kindled by a certain transitory compunction, come as far as confession; they purpose to enter upon the ways of a better life, and promise that thereafter they will not return to the vices they have committed. But because they will not afflict themselves with a satisfaction worthy of what they have committed, 263Anor with the Apostle chastise the body and bring it into servitude (I Cor. IX), when temptation quickly comes upon them they return to the sins which they had forsaken; because they cannot easily resist vices who neglect to mortify in themselves the affections of the vices. Of whom it is here said: Judah hath departed because of affliction and the multitude of servitude. For what does Judah signify but those who confess their own sins? who depart because of affliction and the multitude of servitude, when, overcome, they go away after their own concupiscences; because they dread to mortify them by the affliction and servitude of their own flesh. Against whom Wisdom says: Son, coming to the service of God, stand in fear, and prepare thy heart for temptations (Eccle. II). For those who confess their sins and resolve to amend their 263Blife, these surely come to the service of God. But they neglect to stand there, when, overcome by adversities, they fall from their good purpose. This is what he admonished, prepare thy heart for temptations, that walking in the way of God we may be ready to bear adversities: we who, before we were in the way of God, swelled with pride at not having perpetrated unlawful things. He dwelt among the nations, nor hath he found rest. What is it to dwell among the nations, save to comply with carnal desires, where rest is not found — that rest, surely, which the Lord in the Gospel promises to those who labor, saying: You shall find rest for your souls (Matth. XI); and of which it is said to the sinner: Thou hast sinned, add not again; but be at rest (Eccle. XXI). And deservedly: those who would not endure labor in the body 263Ccome to the labor of the soul, and the fury of evil desires harries the mind of those whose flesh the due penalty does not chastise. So surely that Samson, his eyes plucked out, is set at the mill; because the spirit, having lost the light of truth, is carried round about by the appetite of earthly desires. Which Samson, so long as he had the hair of his head, was unconquerable; but after he fell asleep in a woman's lap, and, shaven as to his head, lost his hair, forthwith he is taken by his enemies, and is blinded, and is even made over to servitude. Samson is interpreted their sun, and signifies the spirit illuminated by divine knowledge. The head of Samson is the principal part of the mind. The hair of the head is the ray of contemplation. The woman's lap is the blandishment of the flesh. For so long as the spirit cleaves to contemplation, 263Dit cannot be overcome by temptation. But if he shall have been dissolved incarnis delight, and shall have fallen asleep there, forthwith he loses the light of inmost truth, and, the ray of contemplation being cut off, he does not suffice to fight back against depraved motions; and at length, his eyes plucked out, he is set at the mill, when, forgetful of the inward sweetness, he is scattered abroad through earthly desires. For what is the mill, save a mind unstable and unquiet which, while it always strives to grasp what it craves, driven round as it were by its own desires, never rests? Well therefore is it said: He dwelt among the nations; nor hath he found rest. Because the mind which follows the desires of the flesh is a stranger to inward quiet in the measure that it is scattered abroad without through things that slip away. All her persecutors 264Ahave taken her, in the midst of straits. Sometimes those who fall away from the purpose of the virtues are worse than those who have not yet been able to come to the purpose of virtue: because the latter hope that they can attain to that which they have not yet had; but the former are so much the farther from salvation as they have the more evidently lost, with despair, the virtue they had begun. More therefore are these open to the snares of the demons than those; because hope of future correction in some manner draws those back, but their own despair drives these to ruin. Therefore of those who, after the beginning of good, return to their vomit, it is here said: All her persecutors have taken her, in the midst of straits. For who are our persecutors, save malignant spirits and the desires of the flesh, which war against us in our 264Bmembers? And what is the straitness, save the despair of a sinful conscience, which straitens the heart within? He therefore is seized by all his persecutors in the midst of straits, who for this reason does not gainsay the demons suggesting sin, and his own desires, in his fault; because from a preceding lapse he already despairs of pardon. Judah therefore hath departed because of affliction, when this man, who by confession had already begun to amend his life, broken by the trouble of temptations, falls from his purpose. He dwells among the nations, when he fixes his mind upon the delight of carnal desires. He does not find rest, when concucupiscentia drags his heart apart through the precipices of the vices. He is seized by all his persecutors in the midst of straits, when, now pressed by despair, he struggles against no 264Cdepraved suggestions.

The epilogue of the whole first alphabet. Our physician, holding the sick man in his hands, behold how he proves the skill of his art. First by long stroking he calls the numbed limbs back to feeling, and by handling the painful wounds with a gentle touch he steadies them for the stroke; then he cuts, then he anoints, then he binds, then he cherishes, and restores him to full health. So this alphabet is divided into five partitions. The first is complaint; the second is rebuke; the third is consolation; the fourth is instruction; the fifth is entreaty. By the complaint he strokes; by the rebuke he cuts; by the consolation he anoints; by the instruction he binds; by the entreaty he cherishes. The complaint is from the beginning of the alphabet as far as that place where it is said: To what shall I 264Dcompare thee (Thren. II), etc. There the rebuke begins, and reaches as far as that place where it is said: The Lord hath done that which he purposed (Ibid.), etc. There the consolation begins, and reaches as far as that place where it is said: Let tears run down like a torrent (Ibid.). In which instruction sinners are formed unto repentance, and it reaches as far as that place where it is said: Behold, O Lord (Ibid.). And from thence to the end is the entreaty, in which the prophet beseeches the Lord for sinners. These things having been briefly tasted beforehand, let us now inspect the order of the complaint itself. First he laments them as though absent, because he ought not so quickly to have disturbed with direct address those who were in bitterness. He begins also to lament first those things which are least, 265Alest the weight of unforeseen grief overwhelm the minds of the mourners; and thus, going forward little by little in his enumeration from the lesser to the greater, he gradually rouses the minds of his hearers to mourning. First therefore he mourns the depopulation of the country; then the destruction of the common buildings; then the overthrow of the sacred edifices; then the contamination of holy things; then from inanimate things he goes forward to the misery of men, namely to the bewailing of their casting down and their want; and so at last he ends his complaint.

(THREN. II.) How hath the Lord covered the daughter of Sion with gloom in his fury. A lamentable beginning: it begins from wonder, that so suddenly was that people cast down which before seemed exalted even to heaven. The gloom signifies the sadness of tribulation. 265BThe daughter, he says, of Sion — Sion herself, that is, the nation of the Jews, whom he loved and guarded with a father's affection; or the daughter of Sion herself, that is, the region of Judea. For the custom of the Scriptures holds that metropolitan cities be called mothers, and the surrounding regions, and the towns too, and the forts and the villages, daughters, Therefore he begins, as was said above, to lament her from afar, namely the region, that at last he may come to the very head of the region, Jerusalem. There follows:

And he remembered not the footstool of his feet. That is, the people, which was humbly subject to his service.

The Lord hath cast down headlong, and hath not spared. Here distinguish.

He hath destroyed all the beautiful things of Jacob in his fury. Here 265Cdistinguish again. Then there follows:

He hath cast down to the ground the strongholds of the virgin of Judah. Here once more distinguish. Then there follows:

He hath polluted the kingdom and its princes. What in the preceding clause he had set forth generally under the name of the land, this he here pursues by parts — the beautiful things, the strongholds, the kingdom, the princes; and see how the lament grows: before he had named the soil only, then the land; but lest perchance that land itself should be thought barren and untilled and therefore less to be lamented, he thereafter strengthens his lament concerning its opulence and its loftiness. He hath cast down headlong, he says: it is more to cast down headlong than to cast away; and what is yet graver, and hath not spared. This was truly fulfilled in the last captivity, because they are now no more to be called back — he hath cast down headlong. 265DWhat: he hath destroyed all the beautiful things of Jacob, he hath cast down the strongholds of the virgin of Judah. By the beautiful things understand opulence: by the strongholds strength and power. By Jacob and the virgin of Judah the same people is signified. Yet Jacob is the general name of the twelve tribes: but Judah pertains to two tribes only. Therefore, the general name having been set first, he at once joined the special name, that he might show that he was lamenting the captivity of the two tribes. Jacob therefore and Judah are the same people: Jacob, because sturdy in power; the virgin of Judah, because delicate in opulence. What then are the beautiful things of Jacob, save the opulence of the strong? and what are the strongholds of the virgin of Judah, save the strength of the opulent. There follows:

266AHe hath polluted the kingdom and its princes. That is, of the virgin of Judah. He hath polluted, he says, because of the nations among whom they were scattered: by whose fellowship and rite they were profaned, who before reigned secure in the protection of God, and trusted in the strength of their own princes. But now the whole kingdom with its princes is polluted, because the people, with its protectors, is subjugated to the nations.

He hath broken in the anger of his fury every horn, and hath at the same time turned back his right hand. By the horn is signified the strength of her princes; but by the right hand of God the divine protection is understood. What then is it that is said of God, he hath broken every horn of Israel, he hath turned back his right hand, save that in a transgressing people he both crushed all human strength, 266Band at length took away his own protection, so that those may be shown wholly desolate from whom, after human defenses, the divine help too is taken away. He hath turned back, he says, his right hand. He turned back the right hand, that he might not protect; and stretched out the left, that he might strike. Whence there follows:

And he kindled in Jacob as it were the fire of a devouring fiammae round about. As fire also, when kindled, ranges widely and consumes all things, so the enemies of the Jews, after all strength had failed in them and the divine help had been withdrawn, with none besieging them, laid all things waste.

He hath bent his bow as an enemy. In the bow a threatening is understood. To bend the bow therefore is to enlarge the threatening or to defer it; but he who strikes after 266Cthe threatening has bent the bow as an enemy. Whence there follows:

He hath made firm his right hand as a foe. When in Scripture the right hand of God is set down, sometimes protection, sometimes a grievous striking is signified, in that the right hand is by natural aptitude the readier for striking. First therefore God turned back the right hand, that he might not protect; afterwards he made the right hand firm, that he might strike. But that it is said, he made firm his right hand, expresses the grievous indignation of him who strikes. Or he makes firm his right hand over the stricken, because the blow which once in anger he inflicted he does not purpose to heal any more; which is properly referred to the last captivity. Whence fittingly is joined, as a foe, who namely strikes unto utter destruction, 266Dnot unto correction. For he would not make firm his right hand as a foe, if, while that one strikes to destroy, he himself were striking to correct. Whence it is to be pronounced with grievous sorrow: as an enemy, as a foe. He, whose wrath the guilty cannot escape, nor can an enemy hinder his mercy.

And he hath slain all that was beautiful to see in the tabernacles of the daughter of Sion. Nothing is slain save what lives. Above he had said: He hath destroyed all the beautiful things; but here, wishing now to say something further, he says, he hath slain all beautiful things, that he may show that by this calamity he took away not only riches, but the very souls themselves. Whence it is rightly subjoined when it is said:

He hath poured out his indignation like fire. Not like 267Awater, but like fire. For the violence of waters is wont to cast down and lay waste things, not to consume them. Whose inundation the Lord threatens through another prophet, saying. I will bring the waters of the flood upon the earth, to destroy all flesh wherein is the spirit of life (Gen. VI). But fire not only destroys, but consumes. And therefore rightly is the indignation of God said to be poured out like fire, when the divine vengeance ranges even to consuming. Lastly, in the very name of pouring out there is expressed the violence and, so to say, the abundance of the tribulation.

The Lord is become as an enemy: he hath cast down Israel headlong; he hath cast down headlong all her walls. Thus far he seems to have lamented the desolation of the daughter of Sion, that is, of the region of Judah. Now he turns himself to the bewailing of the mother Sion herself, namely 267Bthe city of Jerusalem, saying: The Lord is become as an enemy — a grievous grief. The Lord, who was wont to cherish, who was wont to love, is become as an enemy, because he now strikes not as a father unto correction, but as a foe unto consuming. He hath cast down headlong her walls, that is, Sion's; he hath laid waste her strongholds. Walls are made in a city for adornment, strongholds for safeguard. What then is it, he hath cast down headlong the walls, he hath laid waste the strongholds, save that he cast down what was lofty and broke what was strong? What therefore he had lamented above concerning the region, this same thing he now bewails as done in Jerusalem also; so that there, by the beautiful things of Jacob, you may understand the beauty of the region to be signified; here, by the walls of Sion, the adornment of the city; there, by the strongholds of the virgin of Judah, the strongholds of the region; here, by the strongholds of Sion, the strongholds of 267CJerusalem herself. Whence quite fittingly he set there the beautiful things only, here the walls, because the adornment of a region is one thing and that of a city another. For the adornment of a region is rather considered in the richness of its crops and the fecundity of its flocks. The adornment of a city, in the loftiness of its buildings; and therefore the beautiful things are said to be destroyed and the walls to be cast down headlong, that you may know all things laid waste — both those, namely, which adorned the city within by their beauty, and those which adorned it without by the usefulness of the regions.

And he hath filled in the daughter of Judah the humbled man and the humbled woman. The desolation of the region and of the city having been described, as though answering to both he brings in: And he hath filled in the daughter of Judah, that is, in the Jewish nation, which he had loved as a daughter with a father's 267Deffect, the humbled man and the humbled woman, that is, he filled her with the humbled of either sex, so that she should be full of the humbled, that is, of the cast down. Or by the humbled man we can understand the order of prelates; by the humbled woman, the subject people. Corresponding to which he had said above, in the laying waste of the region: He hath polluted the kingdom and its princes, so that we may understand the same by the kingdom as by the humbled woman, the same by the princes as by the humbled man.

And he hath laid waste his tent as a garden. Now he comes to the bewailing of the destruction of the sacred edifices. And so far as concerns the letter, by the tent and the tabernacle he signifies the temple of the Lord which was in Jerusalem, which was overthrown first by the Chaldeans, then by the Romans. And he calls the temple a tent 268Aor a tabernacle for this reason, that in its stead the children of Israel of old made use of a tent and a tabernacle for the worship of God, first in the desert and afterwards in Shiloh. Moreover a tent is properly so called where curtains are stretched by cords to stakes fixed in the ground. But a tabernacle is constructed with certain boards, as is written of the tabernacle of the covenant which Moses built in the desert, over which veils were spread outside and curtains stretched on every side. It is said therefore of the overthrow of the temple, he hath laid waste his tent.

He hath demolished his tabernacle. And according to the letter the words are fitting, that what is stretched out should be laid waste; what is fixed to the ground should be demolished by a kind of undermining. As a garden, he says, 268Bhe hath laid waste his tent. Which Isaiah had foretold before in these words: The daughter of Sion shall be left as a shade in a vineyard, and as a hut in a garden of cucumbers (Isa. I). As if he should say: As from a garden and from a vineyard, after the fruits have been gathered, the guard of men is taken away, so from this people, because it is barren of the fruit of good work, the divine guard shall be taken away, so that it be given over to the laying waste and the plundering of its enemies. Or, by the garden, we can understand a place of pleasure and luxury. Whence it was forbidden by the law that a grove be planted in the courts of the Lord, because the worshippers of idols too were wont to sacrifice under leafy trees and in verdant places, serving wantonness and pleasure. He says therefore, he hath laid waste his tent as a garden, as if he should say: Because into a place of sanctity 268Cunchastity and pleasure entered, therefore laying waste and desolation came. There follows:

The Lord hath delivered to oblivion in Sion the festival and the sabbath. That is, he not only cast these away, but after casting them away seeks them no more. Which properly pertains to the last captivity, when, grace being now made manifest, the observances of the former law were utterly overthrown and repudiated. There follows:

And to reproach and to the indignation of his fury the king and the priest. It is understood: he hath delivered. To a king honor is owed; in a priest devotion is loved. And now, instead of honor, reproach is rendered to the king, and instead of devotion, indignation to the priest, that all the excellence and religion of the Jewish people may be shown destroyed. There follows:

268DThe Lord hath cast off his altar. Here he now begins to treat of the contamination of holy things. The Lord hath cast off his altar: he shows himself to be implacable, when he casts away even that whereby he was wont to be appeased. There follows:

He hath cursed his sanctification. That is, the sacrifices by which they were formerly wont to sanctify those who offered them. Which we now plainly see fulfilled, when the legal victims, which in their own time could sanctify those who offered them, if they be held to after the fulfilling of grace, now the rather provoke God to wrath. There follows:

He hath delivered into the hands of the enemy the walls of her towers. That is, of the sanctification; for that he might show that he no more 269Areceived those former sacrifices, he permitted the very place in which they had been wont to be offered to be overthrown from the foundations, so that from this it might become clear to all that, the former place being made desolate, the former rite ought to cease. But that he says, the walls of the towers, so far as concerns the letter is to be understood thus, as if he should say, her fortified towers, intransitively, that is, not only the walls of the city without, but also the walls of the towers within. Whence there follows:

They have made a noise in the house of the Lord as in the day of a solemn feast. The enemies themselves, namely, with none resisting them, seized upon all things, and rejoiced that they had prevailed.

The Lord hath purposed to lay waste the wall of the daughter of Sion. Lest God should seem to have brought in these so great evils without consideration, he is said to have purposed, that is, deliberated, before the deed. 269BBy the wall of the daughter of Sion we ought to understand the strength and the defense of the Jewish people. There follows:

He hath stretched out his line. That he might measure their merits, and render to each according to his own works. Or he stretched out the line, that is, the penalty. wherewith he should bind sinners, he drew out long, waiting through patience; but striking at last so much the more grievously, wherefore there follows:

He hath not turned away his hand from destruction. That is, because he found none free from fault, he left none exempt from penalty. There follows:

And the outwork mourned, and the wall is laid waste. So ambiguously is it placed, alike, that it seems able to be referred to either, namely either that the outwork was laid waste alike 269Cwith the wall, or that the wall mourned alike with the outwork. And we can not unfittingly understand by the outwork the guard of men, but by the wall the guard of angels, both of which were taken away from the people of the Jews by the just judgment of God, so that neither men without nor angels within could defend from the pressing tribulation the people forsaken by God; according to which acceptation the distinction is fitting, that he bewails the outwork to have mourned and the wall to have been laid waste — so that he may show, namely, the former, that is, the good prelates, troubled by compassion even at the overthrow of their own people, but the latter, that is, the angels, withdrawn from their guardianship.

Her gates are sunk into the ground. That is, Sion's. For 269Da memorial of perpetual grief, after the overthrow of all things the signs of the ruin remain. For the gates remain not for a defense, but for a sign of the calamity; which, that they may lie wide open, are fixed in the ground, and, lest they should furnish a protection, having lost and broken bars, they have no bolts. There follows:

Her kings and her princes are among the nations. It is understood: he hath destroyed and crushed them: and it looks back to what he had said: The gates and the bars (Ezech. XXXVIII), that is, the kings and the princes, who were the defense and the strength of the people, have been cast down and cast away and crushed. There follows the cause of so great an evil, because:

The law of the Lord is not with them. That is, because they are transgressors of the law, and because:

Her prophets have not found vision from the Lord. 270AThat is, because they listened to those prophets who did not speak from the Spirit of the Lord.

How hath he covered, etc. Under the appearance of one nation he bewails the fall of the whole human race. The first man was stricken in three ways: with ignorance, with concupiscence, with mortality. And by mortality is understood death itself, together with all the defects and penalties that run before death itself. He says therefore: How hath the Lord covered the daughter of Sion with gloom in his fury; because man, whom he had created to contemplate the light of eternity, deserting him when he sinned, he left in the darkness of ignorance: for Sion is interpreted a watchtower, where the incursion of enemies is descried from afar. And to the first man it was said: Of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil eat not: for on whatever 270Bday thou shalt eat of it, thou shalt die the death (Gen. II). Man therefore is set up as it were upon a certain watchtower, when he is bidden to beware by circumspection of the peril of the impending evil. What then ought we to understand by the daughter of Sion, save the soul of man not yet strengthened by the practice of circumspection? For, as there is said a daughter of Babylon, that is, a daughter of confusion, and a daughter of Jerusalem, that is, a daughter of contemplation, so there is said a daughter of Sion, that is, a daughter of watching, as one still tender and new in watching, and not yet made solid into manly strength. As if he should say: This at least might have mitigated the fury of him who judges, that he who had transgressed had not yet been made solid in the precept by the practice and experience of obeying. How hath he covered, he says: what is covered is indeed hidden, not taken away; because the light of reason 270Cin man was darkened by sin, not taken away. How hath he covered, texitne lest it should be able to see the higher things, that is, the heavenly. There follows: he hath cast down from heaven the glorious land of Israel. Israel is interpreted a man seeing God. And the first man, before he sinned, was Israel, because through the presence of inward contemplation he saw God. By the land of Israel therefore we fittingly understand the body of man; which God indeed took from the earth by creation, but by immortality transferred in a manner to heaven; and because, sin demanding it, he again bade it be dissolved into death: he cast down the land of Israel as it were from heaven, that is, from incorruption to corruption. And rightly does the prophet, in lamenting the fall of man, commemorate first 270Dthe gloom of the mind, and then the corruption of the flesh; because that ought deservedly to go first in the penalty which went first in the fault: so that, as the flesh would not have come into unlawful delight unless the mind had first been corrupted, so likewise, unless the mind had first been blinded by ignorance, the flesh would not have descended into corruption; because Adam himself too first, while he was being sought, hid himself, and then, having been found, received the sentence of death. There follows:

And he remembered not the footstool of his feet in the day of his fury. By the feet of the Lord his precepts are understood; by the traces of the feet, the knowledge of the precepts. By the footstool of the feet are understood those who by obedience are made subject to the precepts of God; as it were therefore did God set his feet upon a footstool, when upon the first man 271Ahe laid the precept of life to be obeyed; but of this footstool in the day of his fury he was as it were not mindful: he who with great strictness exacted the penalty of transgression from him to whom before he had gently given the precept of obeying.

The Lord hath cast down headlong, and hath not spared. A casting down headlong pertains to a great and to a sudden ruin. See how great a ruin: from paradise into this world, from this world into hell, from immortality into death, from death into damnation. Whence well is it joined: And hath not spared. For he would as it were have spared the sinner if he had tried his guilt by penalty, but had not compelled him to go into corruption through the death of the flesh; or if, after the death of the flesh, he had not at the least cast the soul too headlong into damnation. There follows: 271BHe hath destroyed all the beautiful things of Jacob in his fury, Jacob is interpreted supplanter. And man in a manner supplanted the devil not by fighting but by grace, when he receives the power of ascending thither whence the devil had fallen. This Jacob in the first condition of his being was beautiful within and without, whose conscience flourished through innocence and whose flesh through chastity. But the beautiful things of Jacob were destroyed; because concupiscence both polluted the comeliness of chastity, and the guilt of his own crime made his conscience at once foul and dark.
