Or he had the beautiful things of Jacob because within, the sight of invisible goods gladdened him, and without, the beauty of visible things roused him to the glory of his Creator. But the beautiful things of Jacob were destroyed, because God, in vengeance for the first guilt, hid from 271Chis mind the knowledge of invisible goods, and changed the sight of visible things from that spiritual gladness into the concupiscence of the flesh. Whence well is it joined: He hath cast down to the ground the strongholds of the virgin of Judah. For what do we understand by the virgin of Judah, save the chaste intention of the soul: which intention, namely, when it is everywhere directed to the judgment of the divine will, what else does it do than confess to the Lord? Of which intention the strongholds were the good desires of the soul and the natural appetites of the body. For so long as neither the flesh without nor the soul within craved anything inordinate, the intention of man was in every action as it were fortified and secure. But when the natural desires degenerated into enticing appetites and earthly affections, the strongholds 271Dof the virgin of Judah were as it were cast down to the ground, so that now the intention of man is not secure even in good things, since it can easily be corrupted and deceived from close at hand by inordinate desires. Whence it is subjoined: He hath polluted the kingdom and its princes. The kingdom of the soul was the flesh, so long as the soul had God reigning within it through love. The princes of the kingdom were the virtues of the soul, by which the soul ruled the motions and the appetites of the flesh. But after the spirit swelled against God, straightway it found in its own flesh the contumacy of contradiction; because by God's disposing it was brought about that the cleanness, of chastity should pass into the pollution of concupiscence, so that where before he gloried as humble, there he should blush as proud. Its kingdom therefore, that is, the soul's, and its princes, 272Anamely the kingdom's, God polluted; because out of the concupiscence by which the integrity of the flesh was violated, he suffers the virtues of the soul also to be stained.

He hath broken in the anger of his fury every horn of Israel, etc. By the horn is signified the strength of virtue; by Israel, man; by the right hand, the protection of God; by the enemy, the devil; by the face of the enemy, the pressing on of the devil; by the fire, concupiscence; by the circuit, the body which surrounds the soul. A horn is not born with the animal, and yet there is naturally in it the cause from which the horn may arise. So the soul has its virtues not from nature but from grace; which virtues nevertheless the same grace works in man not without the natural consent of free will. But because man by sinning lost the liberty of choice, and God justly withdrew his grace from sinning man, 272Bevery horn of Israel was as it were broken; because he both lost the virtues he already had, and did not deserve to obtain even those which he was to have. There is also another thing which we can note in the horn. For the horn in growing hardens out of the moisture of the flesh, and the rational creature, grace cooperating, receives strength from the advancement of the virtues. Likewise, as the horn defends the head and the feet, so virtue guards the mind within, and the works without, from harm. He hath turned back, etc. The devil followed the first man walking in the way of obedience, that he might call him back; but he could not have access to harm the man, because the right hand of God was between him and the man. But when man of his own accord turned himself to the persuasion of the devil, straightway God turned back his right hand from the face of the enemy, 272Cso that he should not repel him, now pressing on, from harming the man; that the transgressor might feel the fault of his own transgression at least in the penalty. There follows: And he kindled in Jacob as it were a fire, etc. The fire is the concupiscence of the flesh, which we contract in being born; and so long as we live in this concupiscence, we bear with it as though carrying a certain household foe within ourselves. This fire is always in us, but it does not always burn, it does not always have a flame. But then is the fire kindled into flame, when the tinder of sin is roused as far as delight and consent. Which flame, namely, devours round about; because as the moth consumes a garment, so too depraved delight corrupts the integrity of the flesh with which the soul is clothed. It devours round about, because through all the senses 272Dof the body it pours in on every side the venom of corruption for the violating of the soul's chastity; or by the circuit we can understand this world. And the concupiscence of the flesh devours round about; because running through all the delights of this world, it labors to satiate its own evil desires. He hath bent his bow, etc. In the bow a threatening is understood: in the right hand made firm a vehement striking is marked. God therefore both bends the bow and makes firm his right hand, because to sinning man he both threatens perpetual penalties in the future, and meanwhile avenges his guilt with present scourges as well. Which nevertheless, because he works it not out of anger but out of mercy, he is called not an enemy but as an enemy, and as a foe. Which can also be read otherwise. God bends the bow 273Awhen he threatens sinners with penalty, and yet still defers the sentence by waiting: but because he defers with some that, being corrected, they may be amended, and with others that, persevering, they may be more grievously punished — in those whom he awaits unto amendment he bends the bow, not as an enemy, but as one propitious; but in those whom he suffers to live unto the heaping up of their damnation, he bends the bow as an enemy. Likewise in those whom he corrects by scourges he makes firm his right hand, but not as a foe; but those whom he strikes unto utter destruction, in them he makes firm his right hand as a foe.

But that it is said, he made firm his right hand, can be understood thus. For upon the first man God laid as it were his right hand, when he inflicted upon him as he sinned the stroke of mortality; which right hand God still confirms, 273Bas often as he adds daily scourges upon the first wound. But that he said above. He hath turned back his right hand, and here says, he hath made firm his right hand, is not contrary. For he turned it back, that he might not protect; he turned it toward them, that he might strike; he made it firm, that he might multiply the scourges, so that the present penalty may teach the stricken what manner of penalty awaits in the future those who shall have persevered in evil. Whence it is said in the psalm: Thou hast given a warning to them that fear thee, that they may flee from before the bow, and that thy beloved may be delivered (Psal. LIX). There follows: And he hath slain all that was beautiful to see in the tabernacles of the daughter of Sion. The tabernacle of the soul is the body, because it inhabits it by making it sentient. The tabernacle of the soul is also the conscience, because in it each one rests with himself 273Cand with the household of his own thoughts. All therefore that was beautiful to see in the tabernacles of the daughter of Sion the Lord slew, because he both made all the vigor and beauty of the human body without to wither away from that state of immortality, and within stripped the conscience of man of the honor of right thoughts. Or by all that was beautiful we can understand without, discipline and the comeliness of good works, and within (as has been said) the cleanness of good thoughts. And note that nothing is slain save what lives. Good works live, when they are done with right intention; good thoughts live, when they feel the affection of inmost love. But care must be taken, when God is said to destroy and take away the virtues from man, that he be believed to do this not by acting, but by 273Dpermitting. For he does it, because by a just judgment he permits it to be done. There follows: And he hath poured out his indignation like fire. In the pouring out abundance is signified; as if he should say, he did not drip it, but poured it out, like fire, not like water, but like fire. For fire consumes hard and strong things; like fire therefore is the indignation poured out, because in vengeance for the sinner's transgression not only the flesh but the soul also is tormented. Or because fire hardens mud, but either liquefies or reduces to ashes what is hard: the indignation is poured out like fire, so that in one and the same penalty the hard and the rebellious may be confounded and perish; but those who have been willing humbly to acknowledge their own infirmity may be strengthened and made safe.

The Lord is become as an enemy. We have made 274Athe reason why he should be an enemy, who before was not an enemy, because he hates not that which he himself created, but that which we have made. There follows: He hath cast down her ramparts; he hath destroyed her fortifications. The ramparts of the soul are one thing, her fortifications another. The ramparts pertain to comeliness, the fortifications to safekeeping. So there are certain gifts of graces which adorn, as is prophecy, kinds of tongues, the interpretation of speeches; and certain which fortify, as are faith, hope, charity. The former, if they be present, furnish an ornament to the virtues; the latter, if they be lacking, a peril. The former, even if they be lacking, do not for all that hinder salvation; without the latter men cannot attain to salvation. And well is it first said: He hath cast down the ramparts, and then subjoined: he hath destroyed the fortifications; because, when a sinful soul is abandoned, first 274Bthere are taken from her the gifts which were given for manifestation, and then the foundations of faith, hope and charity are overthrown. He hath cast down, he says, the ramparts. What is lofty is cast down; what is solid is destroyed: because, as has been said, the ramparts pertain to glory, the fortifications to protection. There follows: And he hath filled in the daughter of Juda the humbled man and the humbled woman — that is, he humbled the spirit and the flesh in man by filling them with misery and grief, so that they who would not be humbled well through virtue might be humbled through affliction. Or by the humbled man and the humbled woman we may understand sense and thought, so that she is driven with reproaches within and without, until, confounded, she return to penitence. This may also be referred especially to the Jewish people. For the prophet, considering how 274CGod, after the casting out of the first man, willing to have mercy upon the human race, chose this people alone, that in it he might prepare the beginnings of our salvation; how he tended it with the commandments of the law that was given, and everywhere exalted it by his own protection; and after all these things cast it off when it sinned and transgressed — and so, reckoning a second to the first adjection, he laments, saying: The Lord is become as an enemy, he hath cast down her ramparts, he hath destroyed her fortifications. For when he took away from the people of the Jews prophecy, and doctrine, and the signs of miracles, he cast down as it were her ramparts. But when he permitted them to be blinded through unbelief, and to be strangers from the hope of his permission and of his inheritance, he destroyed as it were her fortifications. There follows: 274DAnd he hath filled in the daughter of Juda the humbled man and the humbled woman. What shall we understand by the humbled man, but the order of prelates; and what by the humbled woman, but the subject people? For both were humbled at the coming of Christ, because the truth which they had preached and believed was to come, they denied when it was present. Concerning whom it is further subjoined.

And he hath destroyed his tent as a garden, etc. The tent is without, the tabernacle within: as also the tabernacle of the covenant, into which Moses entered to consult the Lord. Rightly therefore by the tent is signified the people, by the tabernacle the order of prelates. For when God so exalted that ancient people with temporal glory, he spread out as it were a tent for comeliness. But when, enlightening certain 275Aof them unto the knowledge of his secrets, he held them worthy also of his familiar converse, he built as it were a tabernacle to dwell in. But because afterward, coming in the flesh, he both deprived that people of the knowledge of the truth, its sins requiring it, and then scattered it by the Romans among all the nations of the world, rightly is it now said: He hath destroyed his tent, he hath demolished his tabernacle. And it is to be noted that he says the tent is not demolished but destroyed, and asserts that the tabernacle is not destroyed but demolished. For what is demolished is pulled down gradually and secretly. What therefore in this place is expressed by demolition, but hidden hatred? The tabernacle therefore was demolished and the tent destroyed, because unless envy had driven the Scribes and Pharisees, the teachers of the people, 275Bto the slaying of the Saviour, the vengeance of captivity would not have destroyed that people so cruelly. And see by what likeness he says the tent was destroyed: as a garden, he says. As a garden is laid waste when, its fruits having been gathered out of it, the hedge is destroyed and the guard removed, so from that people, after it ceased to bear the fruit of justice, the guard and protection of God at once withdrew; according to the prophecy of Isaias saying: The daughter of Sion shall be left as a shade in a vineyard, and as a hut in a cucumber-field. And the Lord himself in the Gospel speaks to the Jews, saying: The kingdom of God shall be taken away from you, and shall be given to a nation yielding the fruit thereof (Matth. XXI). For the barren garden, and not bearing fruit, that is the Synagogue of the Jews, is laid waste; and the garden bearing 275Cfruit, that is the Church of the Gentiles, is fortified and enclosed, as is said in the Canticle of Canticles: A garden enclosed, my sister, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed up (Cant. IV). And well does he call her sister whom he names an enclosed garden, because the Church of the Gentiles was born by faith out of the Synagogue, from which also Christ took flesh: yet nevertheless that same Synagogue, because it had not the fruit of justice, he laid waste as a barren garden. The mother therefore of Christ, that is the Synagogue, is a garden laid waste. The sister of Christ, that is the Church, an enclosed garden. There follows: The Lord hath delivered over to oblivion in Sion the festival and the sabbath. For the observances of the law were certain shadows of things to come; and therefore, after the 275DTruth itself came, God no longer admits the former things unto his worship. For he calls him as it were forgetful, because he no more commands those things to be rendered to him. There follows: Unto reproach and unto the indignation of his fury, the king and the priest. Understand: he hath delivered them over. By these two persons was that people governed, namely the legal and the priestly. And the words are fitting: honour to the king, devotion pertains to the priest; but now against honour is set reproach, against devotion indignation. And by their sacrifices God is not appeased, but is angered. As it is said by Isaias: The blood of he-goats, and of calves, and of rams I would not: incense is an abomination to me. When you came before my sight, who required these things at your hands? (Isa. I.) And by the Psalmist: I will not take, 276Ahe says, calves out of thy house, nor he-goats out of thy flocks. Offer to God the sacrifice of praise, and pay thy vows to the most High (Psal. XLIX).

But if we turn the things said above to the moral sense, by the tent we may not unfittingly understand good action, which is spread abroad without for comeliness. But by the tabernacle, conscience, in which each one abides with himself in a certain familiar and domestic converse. For that the tabernacle signifies conscience, the Psalmist makes plain when he says: The voice of rejoicing and of salvation is in the tabernacles of the just (Psal. CXVII). For the just utter their voice as it were in tabernacles, because concerning their good acts they wish to become known within, in conscience, to God alone. As it were a tent, therefore, is destroyed 276Bwhen the brightness of a good work, shaken by the wind of flattery, is driven from its state of uprightness. But the tabernacle is demolished when the cleanness of conscience is secretly corrupted by unlawful delight. And rightly is it said: He hath destroyed his tent as a garden. For in this each one begins to be barren, that he seeks to glory in his good work in the eyes of men, so that for the rest he is now unworthy of God's protection for working well, because he does not keep with God the fruit of his own seed, that is the reward of his own work. This too we may note in the tent: that as a tent is first stretched out by cords, and then those cords are held by stakes fixed in the earth, so also good working ought to be stretched by the perseverance of intention, lest it be slackened; and that intention itself, lest it grow slack from its purpose, 276Cought to be confirmed by the hope of eternal reward, as it were by certain stakes fixed in the solidity of the earth. There follows: The Lord hath delivered over to oblivion in Sion the festival and the sabbath. What is understood by the festival, but inward joy; and what by the sabbath, but the rest of the mind? For he had said two things above, namely: he hath destroyed his tent, and he hath demolished his tabernacle. To these two he seems to answer. As if he said: Because he hath destroyed his tent, therefore he hath delivered over to oblivion the festival. But because he hath demolished his tabernacle, therefore he hath delivered over to oblivion the sabbath. For how great is the sweetness of inward joy he does not remember, whoever seeks joy in the praises of men and in transitory things; and in no wise 276Ddoes the mind enjoy true peace within, when it is troubled by the incentives of vices and by the affections of carnal desires. There follows: Unto reproach and unto indignation the king and the priest. Our soul ought to be both king and priest. King, because it ought to rule its own flesh. Priest, because it ought unceasingly to offer to God its own devotion. But the king is delivered over unto reproach when the soul, made subject to her flesh, is servant to base desires. The priest is delivered over unto indignation when the obstinate mind is hardened by the habit of sin away from that devotion which it was wont to have toward God. God therefore destroys us, demolishes us: he is said to deliver us over unto oblivion, unto reproach, unto indignation, because the afflicted mind complains that this is done as it were by God, which by him, his own judgment 277Apermitting, is not hindered. For he permits that we be destroyed, that being destroyed we forget him, that having forgotten him we come unto reproach and unto indignation. There follows:

The Lord hath cast off his altar. Above he had spoken of the blinding of the Jews; now he subjoins in what order the same was done, saying: The Lord hath cast off his altar. For what is the altar of God, but Christ? Because when through him the mediator God is rendered appeased toward the human race, the sacrifice of our propitiation is as it were offered to God upon him. This altar God the Father as it were cast off, when in the passion he abandoned Christ, even unto the gibbet of the cross, into the hands of the persecuting Jews. God the Father also cursed his own sanctification, because upon him 277Bwhom he had singularly sanctified, and had sent to sanctify us, he first laid the penalty of our curse to be expiated, and then made us, absolved from the guilt of the curse, partakers of that sanctification. There follows: He hath delivered into the hand of the enemy the walls of her towers. The walls of the towers, which in a city are the more eminent, designate the apostles, who then in holy Church were both more eminent in dignity and stronger in constancy. But these too were delivered into the hand of the enemy, because, when they saw Christ die, they in a manner fell through unbelief. There follows: They have made a noise in the house of the Lord as in the day of a solemn feast. By the house of the Lord the Church is understood. The enemies therefore made the voice of exultation in the house of the Lord, when, the shepherd himself being dead and the sheep scattered 277C, they gloried that they had utterly prevailed. Or by the house of the Lord we may understand Christ himself, as the Apostle says: God was in Christ, reconciling the world to himself (II Cor. V). And so they made the voice of the enemy in the house of the Lord when they insulted him as he hung upon the cross, saying: He saved others; himself he cannot save. If he be the Son of God, let him now come down from the cross, and we believe him (Matth. XXVII). Whence it is well added, as in the day of a solemn feast. For a solemnity is a general and common festival. As it were therefore in the day of a solemn feast do they make a noise, who, their joy being fulfilled, exult that nothing now remains over for their desires. The Lord hath cast off his altar. The altar of God in us is our faith, upon which we immolate the sacrifice 277Dof good work. Which altar, namely, is then cast off, when our faith, stripped bare of good works, is reprobated by God. Concerning which it is further subjoined: He hath cursed his sanctification. For our faith is sanctified when merits are increased to it by the increments of virtues; the sanctification is cursed when, the virtues being lost, faith itself is violated by depraved actions. Whence it is further added: He hath delivered into the hand of the enemy the walls of her towers. For what do the walls of the towers signify, but the more eminent virtues, which are both solid through fortitude and upright through circumspection? The walls of the towers therefore are delivered into the hand of the enemy when, the soul being made subject to him through consent unto iniquity, the adversary lords it even over the highest virtues. Concerning which it is rightly subjoined. They have made a noise 278Ain the house of the Lord. For then do the enemies make a noise in the house of the Lord, when the malignant spirits, admitted within the conscience, no longer knocking from without persuade sin to one who resists, but presiding within command one who consents. Whence beautifully by a likeness it is subjoined: As in the day of a solemn feast. For then does the devil fully exult, when he finds nothing in us that gainsays his will.

The Lord hath thought, etc. God is said after the manner of a man to think upon a deed, lest he be believed to do anything rashly and without deliberation. But the deliberation of God is nothing else than his patience and his justice. Patience, by which he long bears with offenders, that they may be corrected; justice, by which he repays those who persevere with what their merits deserve. Whence it is subjoined: He hath stretched out his line. 278BFor what is it to stretch out a line over offenders, but first diligently to examine the manner and the quantity of the offence, and then, according to the measure of the fault, to moderate the measure of the vengeance? Wherefore it is subjoined: And he hath not turned away his hand from destroying. As if he said: He excepted no one from punishment, because he found no one free from fault. Which may also be read otherwise. The Lord hath thought, etc. For by the thought of God the hidden dispensation of his incarnation may be understood. And because, coming in the flesh, he cast off the Jews by a certain hidden counsel, that he might gather the Gentiles unto the faith, he first as it were destroyed the wall of the daughter of Sion, that is, he took away his protection from the people of the Jews, and so thereafter stretched out the line of his inheritance over the people of the Gentiles. For his line was as it were contracted 278Cwhen it was said of the people of the Jews only: Jacob is the line of his inheritance (Deut. XXXII); but then is the line stretched out when, as has been said, the lot of the inheritance is spread abroad over all nations. But because the blinding of the Jews went before the enlightening of the Gentiles, it is rightly subjoined when it is said: And he hath not turned away his hand from destroying. For as it were, stretching out the line, he would have turned away his hand from destroying, if, gathering the Gentiles, he did not reprobate the Jews. Hence it is that the prophet Isaias, when he beheld in spirit the faith of the Gentiles and the blinding of the Jews to come, said: Thou hast multiplied the nation, thou hast not increased the joy (Isa. IX). For the nation being multiplied, the joy was not increased, because the former 278Dfathers, although indeed they rejoiced over the salvation of the Gentiles to come, yet at the same time grieved over the destruction of the Jews. Whence here also it is rightly subjoined: and it sucked the autemurale, and the wall is destroyed. For what in this place does the bulwark designate but the former fathers; and what the wall, but the Mediator himself of God and men. As it is said by Isaias: Sion the city of our strength, a saviour shall be set therein as a wall and a bulwark (Isa. XXVI). For he, according to the form of the humanity he assumed, is made for us as it were a wall; because those whom he received as believing in him through faith, he also guards by defending them against the assault of malignant spirits. But because the holy Fathers also, for the guarding of the Church of God, keep watch unceasingly by preaching and by example in spiritual vigils, 279Athey stand without as it were a bulwark in the city of God let it therefore be said: The bulwark mourned, and the wall is destroyed together; because thereat all the multitude of the former Fathers grieved, that the faithless people of the Jews receives not the Saviour sent unto them. But that it is said, the wall is destroyed, is said in respect of those from whom he was taken away. He was destroyed for the Jews, that he might be built up for the Gentiles, because faith in him passed over to the Gentiles by this, that Judaea, remaining in faithlessness, reprobated it; as it is said by the Psalmist: The stone which the builders rejected, the same is become the head of the corner (Psal. CXVII).

For by this, that he was reprobated by the faithless Jews, of the Gentiles alike and of the believing Jews, as of two walls running together from opposite quarters into one, 279Bhe was made the head. But that he said, together, may be taken thus: either that the wall mourned together with the bulwark, or that the wall was destroyed together with the bulwark. For neither disagrees with sound understanding. For that the Redeemer of the human race himself also, with the rest of the faithful, wept over the faithlessness and the ruin of that people, the Gospel manifestly declares, where it is said. When the Lord drew nigh to Jerusalem, seeing the city he wept over it and said: For if thou also hadst known, for the days shall come upon thee, and they shall compass thee about, and straiten thee on every side, and beat thee flat to the ground (Luc. XIX). Likewise, that the fellowship of the saints together with its own head was destroyed by them, is openly shown in the Acts of the apostles, where it is related how the Jewish nation 279Cwould not receive the faith of Christ, and cast out his faithful ones from their own borders. The Lord hath thought, etc. What is it that the Lord is said to think before the deed, but that God first long bears with sinners through patience, that he may afterward more strictly exact the fault through justice. Whence rightly is it now said: The Lord hath thought to destroy the wall of the daughter of Sion. For what do we understand by the wall of the daughter of Sion, but the virtues of the soul: And often almighty God, reprobating afterward on account of depraved works those whom he had already adopted as sons through faith and the grace of spiritual gifts in the bowels of his Church, deprives them even of those very virtues which he had already bestowed. But because he is never wont to be so grievously angry with us when we sin, unless 279Dhe has first been long provoked by our iniquities, rightly is it now said: The Lord hath thought to destroy the wall of the daughter of Sion. As if it were said: He deliberates before he strikes, nor does he quickly pronounce sentence, until by subtle consideration he examine the fault. Which is also more evidently explained when it is said. He hath stretched out his line. For what is it to stretch out the line, but subtly to examine the measure of the sin? Or to stretch out the line is to bear long with those persevering in sin — not that he by bearing increases the iniquity, but that by his own just judgment he permits it to be prolonged in us, either by not correcting or by not punishing; which iniquity is called the line of God, because it finds in us wherewith we are bound. Wherefore it is needful that we be always solicitous lest, through the gifts of virtues received, 280Aeither pride lift us up or negligence dissolve us; because perhaps, while we, ill secure, neglect to guard the grace received, we know not how dreadful is that which the invisible judge thinks concerning us: who, although he long bears with us sinning, dissembling indeed yet not unknowing, we ought nevertheless to know that where a long deliberation goes before, a heavy chastisement follows; even as we see that the more attentively a man brandishes his stroke, the more perilously he hurls it. Whence it is subjoined: He hath not turned away his hand from destroying. As if it were openly said: The more he first endured by bearing, the less now does he spare in striking; because assuredly it is exceedingly just that, if we do not draw back our own hands, while we may, from unlawful work, God's hand also in avenging be not turned away 280Bfrom destroying. There follows: And the bulwark mourned, and the wall is destroyed together. If by the wall, as has been said, the virtues of the soul are signified, rightly by the bulwark do we understand the works of the virtues. What is it therefore that, God taking vengeance upon us, the bulwark mourns and the wall is destroyed, but that, his grace withdrawing, both virtue is taken away and the works of virtue are made foolish. For then do our good works mourn, when, virtue being lost, even if they remain for a time, they nevertheless have no merit. There are those who will understand by the wall works, by the bulwark faith; and well is faith said to mourn when good works are destroyed, because without good work faith is not approved with God.

Her gates are sunk into the ground; he hath destroyed and broken her bars. In the gates the loftiness of the kingdom, in the bars 280Cthe strength, is noted. The gates therefore are sunk into the ground: loftiness humbled and cast down. The bars destroyed and broken: strength lost and shattered. How this was done he adds, saying: Her kings and her princes among the Gentiles — understand: the Lord destroyed, that is, scattered, and broke, that is, reduced to nothing. By what desert this also came about he subjoins: There is no law, namely among them, because they keep not the law of the Lord: and her prophets have found no vision from the Lord. With bitterness must it be pronounced, her prophets. As if he said: She loved those more who prophesied false things out of their own spirit, than those who found a vision from the Lord. Her gates are sunk into the ground, etc. What ought we to understand by the gates 280Dand the bars of the city, but the prelates of the people? For the same men are both gates through word, and bars through counsel, and ways through example. Through eloquent word, gates of brass; through firm counsel, bars of iron; through good example, level ways. For they are gates when they lead in unto the faith, through the word of preaching, those placed without. They are bars when they confirm and direct, through the counsel of salvation, those established within through faith. They are ways when they become to such as are already beginning an example of walking rightly through good working. Rightly therefore by the gates of Jerusalem may we understand the Scribes and Pharisees, the teachers of the Jews. Of whom it is here said: Her gates are sunk into the ground. Gates, if indeed they are upright and have solid bars, can according to the time 281Abe shut and opened — shut, namely, to enemies, and opened to friends. But gates loosed from their bars, and taken away, do not fortify the entrance, nor do they withstand those wishing to enter. But gates which, their bars being broken, are delixae in the ground, do not indeed furnish a defence, and yet they hinder those wishing to enter. What therefore are upright gates, but teachers preaching heavenly things, which hang well upon bars, because the word of preaching is made firm out of the counsel of the heart? And what are gates destroyed and taken away, but negligent and lukewarm preachers, whom the Lord rebukes by the prophet, saying: Dumb dogs, not able to bark? (Isa. LVI.) And elsewhere: Thou hast not gone up over against them in the day of the Lord, that you might set yourselves a wall for the house of the Lord (Ezech. XIII). Of whom also 281Bhe had said above: Her gates are destroyed. But here, wishing to intimate something further, he says: Her gates are sunk into the ground. For to such the Lord says in the Gospel: Woe to you, Scribes and Pharisees, because you have taken away the key of knowledge. You yourselves have not entered in, and others who wished to enter you have forbidden (Luc. XI). Let it be said therefore: Her gates are sunk into the ground. For by the earth either the love of earthly things, or the humanity of Christ, or the carnal observances of the law may be understood. Well therefore are the gates said to be sunk into the ground, because the Scribes and Pharisees, the teachers of the people, for this cause that they had fixed their mind upon earthly desires, not only would not receive Christ, but rather, lest they should lose that land, killed Christ. Or they are sunk into the earth, because, regarding in Christ the flesh alone, 281Cthey fell into the snare of faithlessness. Or they are sunk into the earth, because, the spiritual understanding of the law having been transferred to the Gentiles, they themselves remained in carnal observances. Whence it is well subjoined: He hath destroyed and broken her bars. For what do the bars designate but the hidden and strong counsels of the Scribes and Pharisees, by which they took counsel to kill Christ, lest they should lose the land? But he destroyed and broke the bars, because he reduced their counsel to nothing. The counsel was destroyed, because by killing Christ they could not extinguish him. The counsel was broken, because for this they lost the land, that they killed Christ. Wherefore it is subjoined: Her kings and her princes among the Gentiles — understand: God appointed, namely that 281Dthe Gentiles should reign and rule over them. So therefore were the bars destroyed and broken, that they not only paid tribute to the kings of the Gentiles, but also that the prefects and governors of kings dwelt among them and oppressed them, and at length cast them out, rebels, from the land, and scattered them through the whole world. Concerning whom it is further subjoined: There is no law, namely among them, because, even though they have the books of the law, yet because they believe not in Christ, they do not observe the law. But because they still promise themselves that their Christ is yet to come, against this it is rightly answered, when it is said:

Her prophets have found no vision from the Lord. As if he said: What they preach concerning the coming of Christ, falsely flattering themselves, this they have found from themselves, not from the Lord revealing it. Sunk in the ground 282Aare her gates. What do we understand by the gates of the soul, but the senses of the body? Which gates, namely, we then sink into the ground when we incline our very senses to earthly desires. But because the sense without never succumbs to base desires, unless first within the guard of the mind has been broken and destroyed through negligence, it is rightly subjoined, when it is said: He hath destroyed and broken her bars; for he destroys the bars when, good fear being taken away, he leaves the mind wandering and dissolute. He breaks the bars when, the vices entering in, he permits the constancy of the mind to be broken and softened. Whereupon there follows: Her kings and her princes among the Gentiles. For by the Gentiles the vices are signified. The kings therefore and princes of the soul are among the Gentiles when those vices themselves through consent begin 282Bto lord it over her. Or by the kings and princes we may understand the very virtues of the soul, by which she was formerly governed. Kings, because within they preside over the wills; princes, because without they move the mind to act well. And it is to be referred to what he had premised: he hath destroyed and broken her bars, namely the kings and princes, that is, he destroyed and broke the virtues among the Gentiles, that is, he permitted them to be broken and oppressed by the vices. For hence it is that he adds, saying: There is no law. For the mind ought to have a law within, namely the fear and the love of God, and to govern its senses without according to the judgment of this law. Fear, namely, lest it dissolve through wantonness along with the concupiscence of the flesh; and love, lest sloth render it torpid in good work. But if perchance the mind fixes its gates, that is its own senses, 282Cin earthly delights, it is itself a judgment to itself, that, its bars being broken — that is, its virtues lost — it has lost also the law of life within, namely the fear of God and love. To whom it is well further said by way of reproach:

Her prophets have found no vision from the Lord. For after the mind, the governance of the understanding being lost, begins to follow the judgment of the senses, it now shuns those things only which the affection of the senses reports to be evil, and follows those which the affection of the flesh approves. Whence it must needs often be deceived: which, while it considers present delights only, does not avoid future bitternesses. Hence it is that Moses so often runs back to the tabernacle, to consult the Lord; 282Dbecause a man ought rashly neither to seek nor to shun anything without, unless he first discern within, by the judgment of the mind, what is pleasing to the will of God. Or to the perverse soul, which everywhere follows the affection of the flesh, not the judgment of the mind: rightly here by way of reproach is it said: Her prophets have found no vision from the Lord. Because assuredly he casts himself headlong into the pit of destruction, who always considers what is sweet to his own flesh, and attends not to what pleases God.

The ancients have sat upon the ground, they have held their peace, etc. Thus far he has described as it were the destruction of buildings: henceforth he laments the misery and grief of men. The ancients have sat upon the ground, they have held their peace. The sitting designates dejection, the silence stupor. And fittingly 283Aare the ancients, in whom the authority of counsel ought to have been, said to have kept silence; because so great was the calamity that it could be relieved by no counsel. There follows:

The virgins have sprinkled their heads with ashes. Of their own accord they defile the beauty of their comeliness, that by that very squalor without they may show the vehemence of inward grief. And beautifully in tribulation do the ancients keep silence, while the virgins sprinkle their heads with ashes; because evil, when it presses vehemently, turns mature senses into stupor, and wanton ones to impatience: concerning whom it is further subjoined:

They are girded with haircloth. This to the ancients.

They have cast down their heads to the ground. ALLEGORICAL. This to the virgins. They wish to hasten death, who to pressing evils add voluntary afflictions besides. 283BThe ancients have sat upon the ground, they have held their peace, etc. The prophet's mind, considering all the evils of the present life, leading the eye of compunction from the beginning — from the very fall of the first man, unto the blinding of the Jewish people, and then descending through the evils of the present Church — extends its lamentation unto the end of time. For in the foregoing he described buildings pulled down, ramparts destroyed, beginning as it were from the lesser: hence now, advancing as it were to greater things, he laments the misery and grief of men these things therefore, which follow, may not unfittingly be referred to the present Church. The ancients of the daughter of Sion have sat upon the ground, they have held their peace. What is understood by the daughter of Sion, but the present Church: which was born of the Synagogue through faith? The ancients therefore of the daughter of Sion are called the prelates of the Church, 283Cwhose old age is sought not in the number of years, but in the advancement of wisdom and in maturity of manners. And well ancients, because not as little ones set under the law, as it were under a pedagogue. For the ancient people was as it were a little one; because it was nourished by the rod of the law and by earthly promises, until it should climb to a mature sense, that it might serve God not for earthly but for heavenly things. These ancients therefore of the daughter of Sion ought not to sit upon the ground, that is, to seek earthly and transitory things; but to be in the heavens by their converse and their desire, so that what they preach by word they may show by their manners. But because the prophet foresaw that there would be many in holy Church who, obtaining the place of prelacy, would stain by perverse manners the dignity of their office, they will burst forth into a voice of grief, thus 283Dhe says: The ancients of the daughter of Sion have sat upon the ground. For what is it to sit upon the ground, but by leaning upon earthly delights to cease from good work? But because they who, ceasing from good, do perverse things, blush to speak the word of truth lest they become witnesses of their own depravity, he rightly went on and added: They have held their peace; because assuredly, where the guilt of crime gnaws the conscience, the fear of confusion binds the tongue from preaching. There follows:

The virgins of Jerusalem have sprinkled their heads with ashes. What do we understand by the virgins of Jerusalem, but the good subjects in the Church, who, preserving the integrity of faith by sincerity of manners, hasten to the vision of the peace above? And what by the head, but spiritual action? What 284Ais signified by the ash, which is left behind by fire, but earthly thoughts? For ash is born as it were from fire, when the leavings of empty thoughts are generated out of carnal concupiscence. Well therefore, after the ancients sit upon the ground and are silent, do the virgins sprinkle their heads with ashes; because whenever the simple in the Church see their prelates, the zeal of preaching being set aside, leaning upon earthly delights, they too begin to be carnally delighted even in the good things which they do. And rightly did he name the ancients of the daughter of Sion, and the virgins of Jerusalem, because prelates in holy Church ought to be lofty through the providence of circumspection. But subjects, in the good things which they humbly perform, ought always through the intention of the mind to look toward the vision of eternal peace; so that both 284Bthey, being set on high through virtue, may guard the things that are beneath, and these, being placed below through humility, may always strive by intention toward the things above. This therefore the prophet laments, that they sit who ought to have stood upright, and that they who were wont to contemplate heavenly things cast ash upon themselves, that they may not be able to lift their sight to lofty things. And note that even the perfect are said to have dust upon their feet; but they who sprinkle their heads with dust are now numbered among those to be lamented, because in earthly actions the good do indeed sometimes contract light stains through weakness, whereas the evil grow foul, even in those things which they seem to do bravely, through the intention of praise. There follows:

They are girded with haircloth. What is signified by the haircloth, but the care 284Cof earthly things, which pricks the conscience with the continual stings of occupations? First therefore are the ancients described as sitting upon the ground, and afterward as girded with haircloth, because where the mind has first been loosened in the love of carnal delights, presently thereafter care follows, which pricks it by making it solicitous for the fulfilling of its own desires. The virgins likewise are said first to sprinkle their heads with ash, and afterward to cast down those same heads to the ground, because this is the order of destruction: that each one first darkens the good things which he does through the intention of human praise, and then changes even those good works themselves into depraved actions. MORAL. The ancients of the daughter of Sion have sat upon the ground, they have held their peace. By the diverse ages, and sexes, and offices of persons, 284Dthe inward motions of the soul are distinguished. For what the offices of persons distinguish without, this the motion of the affections performs distinctly within. What therefore ought we to understand by the ancients of the daughter of Sion, but the counsels of a faithful soul? But it must be known that the counsels of the elect are one thing, and those of the reprobate another. For the counsels of the elect are to despise the present life and its delights, to keep patience in adversities, through present tribulations to reach per tingere unto future glory. The counsels of the reprobate are to flee adversities, to love the present life, to give tarrying to carnal desires; to despise or to despair of the life to come. Of the counsel of the ungodly it is said by the Psalmist: Blessed is the man who hath not walked in the counsel of the ungodly (Psal. I); and of the counsel of the just, in the same psalm afterward, 285Ait is consequently added: Therefore the ungodly rise not again in judgment, nor sinners in the counsel of the just. If therefore they who are converted to the counsel of the just rise, deservedly are they said to sit who have slipped down into the counsel of the ungodly; because the one tends upward and the other downward, the one seeks heaven and the other earth. The ancients therefore of the daughter of Sion sit upon the ground, when the affections of a soul forgetful of the eternal promise cleave to earthly and transitory delights. Of whom it is rightly said, they have held their peace. For inward desire is as it were a certain cry in the ears of God. And therefore the more a depraved mind seeks transitory things without, so much the more does it fall silent within from the desire of things eternal. There follows: The virgins of Jerusalem have sprinkled their heads with ashes. By the virgins of Jerusalem 285Bwe ought to understand the clean thoughts of the soul. What is it therefore that, after the ancients sit upon the ground, the virgins are said to sprinkle their heads with ash, but that when the affections of the soul have been depraved, straightway a mist of thoughts is generated? Whence it is further subjoined: They are girded with haircloth. For after the sitting and the sprinkling of ash the haircloth follows, because the depraved delight and the confusion of the mind a harsh compunction out of the guilt of its own crime is generated to the sinning conscience. But last of all, at the end, the heap of unhappiness is added, when it is subjoined: The virgins of Juda have cast down their heads to the ground. For to cast the heads to the ground after the girding of haircloth is, after the anguish and trembling of an evil conscience, to despair also of pardon. Wherefore also they are rightly called the virgins 285Cof Juda. For to the increase of grief confession is named last in them, because that too, the last of all, after all the other virtues have been lost, is shut out from the despairing.

My eyes have failed for tears, etc. LITERAL. The prophet, in the person of the Church lamenting her own members, having enumerated the evils of the lost, adds the compassion of the good; for the evil are lamented by the good before they are lamented by themselves; and while the ill-sound still exult in their own destruction, the sighs of others indicate how much they are to be made. He says therefore:

My eyes have failed for tears, my bowels are troubled: my liver is poured out upon the earth. The eyes in holy Church are the provident, the bowels the merciful, the liver the zealous and those fervent in good work. For as 285Dthe natural philosophers report, in the liver is the heat of digestion. As therefore the jecut, or liver, by its own heat digests raw food in the stomach itself, and thence transmits the nutritive juice into all the parts of the body, so assuredly do they who rouse others by the fervour of their devotion from torpor to work well minister nourishment, as it were out of their own digestion, to the body of Christ. What is it therefore that, sin laying waste the faithful people, the eyes of the Church fail, and the bowels are troubled, and the liver is poured out upon the earth, but that they who truly have the charity of Christ always suffer with the miseries of others? As the Apostle says: Who is weak, and I am not weak? who is scandalized, and I am not on fire? (I Cor. XL). For there is no surer sign of true charity 286Athan the affection of fraternal compassion. And well did he say that the eyes fail for tears, because often fault ulcerates the life of subjects with so vehement a blow that all the reason and diligence of prelates succumbs, and now amid such great evils there is no other counsel save only to flee to tears and to implore the divine clemency. This the Psalmist beautifully signifies, saying: They were troubled, and were moved like a drunken man; and all their wisdom was swallowed up. And they cried to the Lord when they were in tribulation: and he delivered them out of their necessities (Psal. CVI). Well therefore do the eyes fail for tears, because often, when evil presses vehemently, sorrow makes the eye of discretion grow dim. The bowels are troubled, because within the affection of piety is shaken. The liver is overspread upon the earth, 286Bbecause often in the elect, through sorrow at another's peril, the alacrity of the mind is bound up, so that they are rendered the more lukewarm to do good things even in themselves, the more they are afflicted in compassion for a brother's tribulation. By the eyes therefore, and the bowels, and the liver, are signified they who lament, not they who are lamented. Whence also presently, joining on a sentence by the words following concerning those who are lamented, he sets forth the cause of grief, saying:

For the destruction of the daughter of my people. For what is signified by the female sex, but the weak common people? whom nevertheless, when he names her daughter, he shows to have the profession of faith; and by the destruction an irreparable calamity is shown, as what is crushed is no more repaired. There follows

286CWhen the little one and the suckling fainted in the streets of the town. He determines the destruction: by the little one, the untaught people and simple in faith. By the suckling, depraved teachers. By the streets of the town we ought to understand the pleasures of this world. The little one therefore and the suckling faint in the streets of the town, when all the carnal and foolish go with perverse teachers along the broad way of pleasures unto destruction; and note that he says, fainted. For those are more to be lamented who fail from good, than those who never were good.

They have failed, etc. ALLEGORICAL.: It is proper to the elect to lament others' evils as their own. For as by rejoicing together with the good they become partakers in their merits, so also by grieving together with the evil through compassion they make gain for themselves out of 286Dtheir destruction. Let the sinner therefore learn how he ought to weep for his own evils, when the just man afflicts himself with so great contrition for the offences of others. My eyes, he says, have failed for tears. How greatly, think you, was that man pricked, who so wept? For he does not show that he poured out tears once only, who says that his eyes have failed for tears. But because many pour out tears and are not pricked in heart, he went on and added: My bowels are troubled, that is, the arrow of grief penetrated even to the inmost parts of the heart, grief touched the sense, and the affection of piety trembled. There follows: My liver is poured out upon the earth. What is signified by the pouring out, of the liver, but the affliction of the flesh? For because in the liver the digestion 287Aof the stomach receives its heat, they who in the affliction of their own body set care for it aside, what else do they but pour out the liver upon the earth. Lest therefore the tears be believed empty, the sighs feigned, the groans simulated, behold, a manifest affliction of the flesh follows. But perhaps this man grieves for parents or kinsfolk. Hear what he says. For the destruction, he says, of the daughter of my people: not of a father, not of a mother, not of a brother, not of a sister, not of a kinsman, not of a relation by marriage, but of my people, he says, lest the goad of his grief should be thought the affection of the flesh and not of charity. But I suppose this man honours with his lament the famous and the rich among the people? Attend to what he says. When there fainted, he says, the little one and the suckling. First of all mark his discretion. He laments that they fail, and does not lament that they are not rich. Who a little one, he says, and 287Ba suckling. See that he has compassion on weakness, and does not venerate strength or pomp. But perhaps he still honours, in the weakness of sex and of age, nobility of birth: In the streets, he says, of the town. Not in a hall, not in a city, not in a commonwealth, not even in a house, but in the streets of the town; because the greater the abjection of the crushed, the heavier, and therefore the more precious, is the compassion of him who grieves.

They have failed, etc. MORAL. What is understood by the eyes, but the clarity of contemplation; and what by the bowels, but the sense of inward sweetness; and what by the liver, but the fervour of divine love? What is it therefore that the prophet records, at the destruction of the daughter of his people, that his eyes have failed, and his bowels are troubled, and his liver 287Cpoured out upon the earth, but that every spiritual man, the more he pours his mind outward unto compassion in the tribulation of his neighbours, so much the more deplores those inward joys, which he was wont to taste in quiet, as withdrawn by that very perturbation of his own sorrow? There follows: For the destruction, etc. By the daughter of the people is understood the carnal soul; by the little one, the sense of the flesh; by the suckling, the virtue of the mind. For the sense of the flesh, so far as in it lies, always seeks certain childish and light things, save in so far as the constancy of the mind restrains it through discipline. As a little one therefore it is fed with milk, because through the virtue of the mind it is nourished little by little unto the maturity of constancy, as unto manly strength. There follows: In the streets of the town. By the town we may not unfittingly understand the body which 287Dthe soul inhabits, and by the streets of the town the flowing desires of the flesh. Why therefore are the little one and the suckling said to faint in the streets of the town at the destruction of the daughter of the people, but that in a carnal soul the sense without is first corrupted through enticing desires, and then the virtue also and constancy of the mind is softened?

They said to their mothers, etc. LITERAL. In mothers is noted a more tender affection of love, who, if they can, not only hear the desires of their sons but anticipate them. Why therefore do they wait for their sons to ask, unless because they have not what to offer to the needy? And lest thou suppose that a mother's tenderness had hardened by some natural vice, hear what they once showed themselves to their sons. 288AThey do not ask for barley and water, that is, servile foods, because they were not wont to give anything of that kind; but:

Where is, they say, the wheat and the wine. This they ask, which they were accustomed to receive. Yet they do not ask, but enquire in wonder, because they were not wont to ask, but to have. But perhaps thou wilt say, these sons were wanton and dainty, they sought superfluous things, which even a mother's tenderness ought to refuse; hear what follows.

When they fainted, he says, as the wounded in the streets of the city. A twofold evil, want and ignominy. It would at least have been more tolerable to faint in the house, and to be consumed with hunger in secret, than in the streets, together with the wasting of hunger, to bear the confusion of ignominy. There follows:

When they breathed out their souls in the bosom of their mothers. 288BAll things work together unto evil. In the streets they faint. In the bosom of their mothers they breathe out their souls, so that both the manifest wasting may multiply confusion, and the present death, grief.

To their mothers, etc. ALLEGORICAL. The prelates of holy Church ought to be ancients in prudence and in counsel, and mothers in tenderness. But because any who are depraved and negligent, when they obtain the place of prelacy, put off the care of their subjects, and study rather their own avarice or their own luxury, rightly is it now said by the prophet: They said to their mothers: where is the wheat and the wine. A great grief, because what they withhold from those who ask, this they ought to have offered even to those who do not ask. But it must be known that in three ways the life of subjects speaks to prelates: through misery, through desire, through obedience. 288CFor they ask when they are in need, they ask when they desire, they ask when they are ready to hear. Through misery they ask, because the neediness of the afflicted, once seen, while it provokes pious minds to succour, even if the tongue be silent, the life cries out. Through desire also they ask, because in a manner to ask is to wish to have. Likewise through obedience they ask, because, while they offer themselves ready to receive, they say as it were that they wish and desire. To negligent prelates therefore, even if not the tongue of their subjects, yet their life always speaks; because both by the peril of the evil and by the desire and the obedience of the good they are moved to bestow the zeal of preaching and the example of a good manner of life. And attend to this: that in the time 288Dof necessity the sons call not upon fathers, but upon mothers. As if they said: If you have forgotten what is due, you ought at least not to forget tenderness. Let the dumb dogs, not able to bark, blush (Isai. LVI). Let them blush, who from shepherds are become wolves. Let them blush, who feed the Lord's flock not as shepherds but as hirelings. Who are strong for tearing and feeble for protecting. Ready to receive, slow to bestow. Eloquent to circumvent, untaught to speak well: who either exact more than is due; bestow less than is due, or nothing; who have changed the judgments of truth into forensic causes. Woe to them, why do they not attend to what they owe; and if they will not anticipate the petition, let them at least hear those who ask, because perhaps even in the petition itself they will be able to learn 289Awhat they owe. Where is, say they, the wheat and the wine? They do not ask for barley and water, but for wheat and wine. Let us see therefore what this wheat and wine may be, and why the sons do not ask for barley and water, although they are set in the necessity of famine. In the Gospel we read that five thousand men were fed with five barley loaves (Joan. VI). But afterward four thousand are satisfied (Matth. XV), and yet nothing is there said about barley loaves. Likewise we read in the Gospel that six stone waterpots were first filled with water, but afterward those waters were changed into wine (Joan. II). Of wheat also the Lord himself makes mention in the Gospel, saying: Unless the grain of wheat falling into the ground die, itself remaineth alone. But if it die, it bringeth forth much 289Bfruit (Joan. XII). What therefore is understood by the wheat, but Christ; what by the barley, but the law; what by the water, but carnal sense; what by the wine, but spiritual understanding? To servants therefore, who held the law by a carnal sense, barley and water could suffice; but the sons, who are now adopted through grace in Christ, and know how to eat Christ spiritually, desire nothing but wheat and wine. All these things, says the Apostle — namely the observances of the law — I count as dung, that I may gain Christ (Philip. III). See in what manner this man casts away the barley of the law, and seeks to eat nothing but wheat, that is, Christ. And well by the barley, which is rough to eat, is the law understood, and by the wheat, which is pleasant and sweet, Christ; because the law 289Ccame to punish sins, and Christ to loose them.

Why therefore Christ should be called wheat, we now by the grace of God both learn by reading and perceive by eating. And in four ways do we eat Christ. For we eat Christ when, by bodily taking the sacrament of his body and blood, we spiritually fatten our souls. We eat Christ when by believing we love Christ. We eat Christ when we imitate Christ. We eat Christ when by hearing the word of God we pass over into the manner of life of the new man. What is it therefore to seek wheat, but to seek Christ, to seek the word of God, the doctrine of truth, and the manner of life of the new man? Wheat therefore we seek 289Dwhen we desire to hear the word of life and to imitate Christ. But if we eat wheat, we ought to drink hibere wine and not water, that is, to take the word of God by hearing, and through spiritual understanding to water it within us. For this is what is commanded us in the law, that we boil not the flesh of the paschal lamb in water (Exod. XII), because that which is given for spiritual feeding ought not to taste carnally to us. Let the good subjects therefore question their evil prelates, and say: Where is the wheat? As if they said: Where is the doctrine of truth? where are the examples of the new man? As if they said more openly: You neither by speaking show forth the truth, nor by living well show yourselves worthy of imitation. Where is the wine? As if they said: You, who handle even spiritual things carnally, how will you be able to call us from carnal things 290Ato spiritual? You, who cannot forget the earthly things which you seemed to have cast away, how will you make us drunk unto the forgetting of the things which we possess? Eat first, and afterward refresh others; be made drunk, and then make others drunk. O the shame! What shall we say to these things? What wonder is it if the people love carnal things, when the clergy sell even spiritual things for carnal? Whence it is rightly subjoined, when it is said. When they fainted as the wounded in the streets of the city. There are two cities, Babylon and Jerusalem. But Babylon has streets, that is, broad ways, which lead to death. In the streets therefore of the city do they faint who, flowing away through the enticements of the flesh, grow soft from the virtue of the mind; or by the city we not unfittingly understand the Church herself; for 290Bholy Church has streets, and has also paths. For what are earthly actions, but certain streets; and what are spiritual actions, but paths? In the streets therefore of the city are they who in holy Church lawfully serve earthly actions. But in the streets of the city they faint who, occupied with earthly business, find no spiritual consolers. Who assuredly would in no wise faint, if the losses which they often suffer without were always repaired within by the counsel and admonition of good prelates. Whence it is well said of such, as the wounded. For what are sins, but wounds? As the wounded therefore do they faint, who tend to destruction through negligence, just as others do through fault. With great grief therefore must it be pronounced, as the wounded, as if it were said: 290CWhy do these perish through the negligence of prelates, who are not wounded by crimes and outrages? Whence there follows: When they breathed out their souls in the bosom of their mothers. For what is the bosom of the mothers, but the soft flattery of prelates? As it were dying; for mothers hold their sons in the bosom, when evil prelates, the minds of carnal men perishing in their own body, not only do not rouse them to good working, but even, stroking them with soft favours, cherish them in their own destruction.

They said to their mothers, etc. MORAL. What are we to understand spiritually by the sons of mothers, unless a multitude of thoughts, which, while they come forth in one mind out of diverse affections, proceed as it were the offspring of many mothers 290Din one house, yet not from one origin? Now we have said that thoughts are born of the affections for this reason, that assuredly the memory of those things comes to us most frequently through thought, with the love of which we have been affected. This we easily prove to be true through the individual vices and virtues. For hence it is that it is said in the Gospel: Where thy treasure is, there is thy heart (Matth. VI). As if it were said: Where thy love is, there is thy mind. Such as thy affection is, such also is thy thought. But it must be known that, just as the affections generate thoughts out of themselves, so those thoughts in turn the more inflame the affections from which they arise. Whence also in those who diligently guard their heart from unlawful thoughts, the roots of evil 291Aaffections at length wither away utterly. But on the contrary those who neglect to exercise their mind in holy meditations, even if they have first grown warm with some spark of divine love, gradually cool, and at last so far that, even if they should sometimes have thoughts of good affections, yet they perceive nothing of inward sweetness distilled from them into the bosom of the mind, not even thinly. For which reason it is rightly said in this place: They said to their mothers: Where is the wheat and the wine? For what is signified by the wheat, unless the food of wisdom; and what by the wine, unless inward joy? For as bodily bread refreshes the belly, so wisdom feeds the mind; and as wine eagerly drunk inebriates, so inward joy estranges the mind from carnal desires. What is it then that the famished sons seek from their mothers 291Bwheat and wine, unless that thoughts born of good affections, but dried up through negligence, cannot find in them the savor of their former sweetness? Of whom it is rightly added: When they fainted as wounded in the streets of the city. For what are the streets of the city, unless the wanderings of the mind? In the streets of the city therefore they faint, because, while the wandering mind does not gather them to inward quiet, they do not reach as far as the taste of the innermost savor. To whom this also well agrees, that it is said as wounded. For depraved desires are the wounds of good thoughts, because, while they infect them by their admixture, they corrupt the integrity of their rectitude like certain blows. Rightly therefore are the barren thoughts of the soul said to faint as wounded, 291Cbecause they remain as empty of the fruit of justice as those which have been corrupted through unlawful desires. There follows: When they breathed out their souls in the bosom of their mothers. The bosom of the mothers is the lukewarmness and sloth of good affections; in which the little thoughts are deservedly said to breathe out their souls, because, when the mind grows lukewarm from the fervor of divine love, presently all the vigor of good thoughts dies away

To what shall I compare thee? etc. LITERAL. Little by little he advances the lament. First he mourned the buildings, and then the men, and these as though absent; now at last, their minds being as it were softened and made accustomed by long weeping, he turns his words to those present, saying: To what shall I compare thee, and to what shall I liken thee? as if he were to say: Because thy contrition is so great, to what shall I compare thee? and, because thou art so 291Dgrievous, to what shall I liken thee? Which is openly to say. Thy evils surpass all misery both in magnitude and in manner. But because great things too can sometimes be compared with the least by a certain differing likeness: he adds: And I shall make thee equal, etc. Which is to say. Other evils, even if they can be compared with thee by a certain difference, yet none can be so by equality. Why? There follows: For great as the sea is thy contrition. It is remarkable that he records that her calamity can be made equal to none, and at once makes the greatness of the sea equal to it in comparison. But it is to be understood thus, as if he were to say: Just as the sea excels all other waters both in bulk and in bitterness, so no calamity can be made equal to thy contrition. There follows: 292AWho shall heal thee? As the water of the sea cannot be dried up, so thy contrition will not be able to be reckoned by man, nor is there any in whom thou mayest hope save God alone. To what shall I compare thee? Those who, after the truth has been acknowledged, go back are worse than those who have never acknowledged the truth. And the more excellent each one's rank is, the more perilous is the fall. Whence the prophet, when he had lamented above the negligence of evil prelates in the Church and the ruin of their subjects, straightway following added.

To what shall I compare thee, and to what shall I liken thee, and make thee equal, O virgin daughter of Sion? The heaping up of words expresses the force of great grief. As if he were speaking to the Church herself concerning her withered members and saying: As formerly, when thou didst hold the faith by living rightly, thy glory 292Bwas comparable to none, so now, when by a reprobate life thou hast fallen from the faith, thy ignominy is comparable to none. There follows:

For great as the sea is thy contrition. ALLEGORICAL. What is understood by the sea, unless the worldly life; which, while it is always drawn by an unstable motion now from adversity to prosperity, now from prosperity into adversity, is tossed as it were by certain surging storms? And because it never excludes either fear in prosperity or grief in adversity, like the sea, into whatever quarter it flows back, it does not lose its bitterness: let it be said therefore to her who grows bitter away from the sweetness of the spiritual life through carnal concupiscence and worldly cares, let it be said, I say: Great as the sea is thy contrition. As if it were openly said: Thou who, through contempt of the world and desire of eternal goods, from carnal love 292Cwast made sweet, how art thou turned again into bitterness? There follows:

Who shall heal thee? MORAL. Let the prelates of the Church hear this, who are appointed physicians of souls; let them hear what is said. Who shall heal thee? As if it were said: Physicians cure other sick men, but who shall cure ailing physicians? If the people sins, the priests pray for it. But for the priests, if they have sinned, who will pray? These things are utterly perilous, and yet true. And though we fear to hear them, yet we cannot deny them.

To what shall I compare thee, etc. The sinful soul is rebuked, whose so great evils have been enumerated above, which, having played the harlot away from the love of God, made her incest the more shameful in proportion as the integrity that fell was the more precious. 292DTo whom it is well said. Great as the sea is thy contrition. For what is understood by the sea, unless a depraved conscience, which both the memory of past crimes and the delight of present ones toss about by driving it hither and thither? For like certain waves coming from opposite quarters they dash against one another, when in a certain way the penitence for her past errors always draws the unhappy soul back, and the love of present ones drives her on. And because in this so great contradiction that sweetness of wretched delight, such as it is, is always infected with grief mingled in, rightly is a depraved conscience called a sea not only for its unquietness but also for its bitterness. There follows: Who shall heal thee? He shows it to be utterly great and difficult, yet not for God 293Aimpossible: that such a one should return to health. Hence it is that John says of him that sins unto death: I do not say for him that anyone should pray (I Joan. V). As if he were to say: Just as I do not forbid desiring his salvation, so I do not presume to command praying for him, because the cure of so mortal a wound, although it does not exceed the power of God, nevertheless surpasses all human estimation; and therefore it is done more considerately if meanwhile, with prayer humbly suppressed, desire only be offered to God, because perhaps in a case so difficult a humble and fearful devotion will obtain more quickly than a presumptuous petition. He says therefore: Who shall heal thee? As if he should say: Perilous is thy wound, which cannot be relieved by human industry, and which on account of past guilt is not worthy to be healed by God. 293B

Thy prophets, etc. LITERAL. It is said with reproach: Thy prophets. As if it were said: Behold to what great misery thou hast sunk by hearkening to false prophets, whom thou didst love, while thou wouldst not hear the prophets of God who spoke true things. They have seen for thee. For thee they saw, because they were thy prophets. Therefore their lie redounded upon thee, because their malice pleased thee. Thy prophets have seen for thee. What did they see for thee? False and foolish things. False, in which thou wast deceived. Foolish, in which thou canst not be excused. If they had seen only false things, in which there would at least have been some likeness of truth, thy simplicity could perhaps have been excused through ignorance; but now, because they saw foolish things also, and yet thou wast not afraid to believe, 293Cthy folly is deservedly punished. But what false things and what foolish things did they see? False takings-up, behold the foolish; false castings-out, behold the false. When and to whom they willed they promised prosperity. When and to whom they willed, they threatened adversity. But both were false, because to those who were to be cast out they promised takings-up, and to those who were to be taken up, castings-out. When God was angry, they announced that he would take thee up to protect thee; when he was appeased, they threatened that he would cast thee out to afflict thee. But the threatening was false, and the promise foolish. In the promise there was not only falsity but also folly, because, even if thou didst fear the terrors, it is utterly alien from reason that thou, conscious of so great a crime, couldst be deceived by empty promises. 293DBehold, thou didst not know what was to come; yet thou wast not ignorant of thy evil desert, which was present. But they, by seeing false and foolish things, did not open to thee thy iniquity, because, while they promised prosperity to the perverse in their crime, and troubled with empty terrors those who walked in simplicity, they did not open, nay rather they veiled thy iniquity, lest thou shouldst acknowledge it. Or it can be distinguished otherwise. What he said, false and foolish, this pertains to the promises; but what follows, false takings-up and false castings-out, both are to be referred to the terrors. For he calls tribulation both a casting-out, in that the people was to be cast out by God, and a taking-up, after the likeness of a burden, which when taken up weighs down the bearer. For hence it is that where the blessed Jerome 294Atranslated the burden of Babylon, or of Tyre, or of Damascus, etc. Symmachus and Theodotion rendered assumptio. And rightly, for the aforesaid reason, he calls the terrors only false; but the promises he names not only false but also foolish, because, even if a man cannot always know what is to come, yet it is exceedingly foolish to be ignorant of oneself in the present. Whence immediately after he had said, they have seen for thee false and foolish things, he subjoins. Nor did they open to thee thy iniquity. For he who promises to a sinner the reward of the just, as it were hides his iniquity, lest it be seen.

Thy prophets, etc. ALLEGORICAL. As it is a fault in evil prelates that through their negligence their subjects perish, so also it is reckoned a fault in the subjects that, 294Bthe good being despised, they imitate the examples of evil prelates and give ear to perverse teachings. Fittingly therefore, after he has censured the perversity of the prelates, he now turns the words of grief by way of reproach to the subjects themselves also, saying: Thy prophets have seen for thee false and foolish things. The prelates of the Church are prophets when, for the instructing of the faith and morals of their subjects, they now unlock the hidden things of the Scriptures, now foretell things to come concerning the rewards of the good or the punishments of the evil. But since all the perverse, in speaking, seek not so much the correction of their hearers as their favor, it is now rightly said of them: They have seen for thee false and foolish things. For they see false and foolish things, because in discerning the morals of their subjects they do not hold the judgment of truth. And when they praise any depraved men in their perversities for the sake of 294Cfavor, they the more befool by flattery those who are foolish through negligence. Whence it is rightly added: Nor did they open to thee thy iniquity, that they might provoke thee to penitence. But because evil prelates sometimes pursue with hatred even innocent subjects, and, while they avenge their own injuries, pretend that they are pleading the cause of God, it is rightly subjoined, when it is said: But they have seen for thee false takings-up, and castings-out.
