As if it were said: To those with whom they themselves had been angry, they announced that divine wrath and vengeance were hanging over them, so that, by calling God the avenger of their own injuries, they might provoke the minds of the people to show reverence to themselves.

294DThy prophets, etc. MORAL. Who are the prophets of the sinful soul, who see false and foolish things for them, unless outwardly the senses of the body and inwardly the crafty thoughts which the providence of the flesh begets? Let us consider a little more attentively how false these prophets are. Behold one of them. How sight deceives by prophesying false things. If thou shouldst see a thing to be desired, what does it foretell to thee? Love, it says, follow, lay hold, enjoy; thou shalt be happy if thou hast gained such a desire. But the unhappy soul, while, ill-credulous, it follows the deceitful promise, is bound in a greater misery through the accomplishment of the crime. So hearing, so smell, so taste and touch prophesy false things, and ensnare those who believe them, because, before concupiscence takes its trial, they promise that all things to come will be prosperous and alluring; 295Abut when the wretched delight, briefly tasted, has passed away, they leave the conscience stunned and full of horror. But these prophets can be caught out in their deceits the more easily, the more manifest, and the more often devoid of reason, is that which they promise. But the craftiness of the heart is the more perilous the more hidden it is, because it not only promises deceitfully but also compels the mind to believe by a certain artful reason for acting. This is the prudence of the flesh which works death, which the wicked sons of this world have, as the Lord says in the Gospel: The sons of this world are wiser than the sons of light in their generation (Luc. X). This prudence begets those perverse prophets, namely the crafty thoughts of the soul, which coax the soul 295Bwith false promises, and seduce and ensnare it when it consents to them. One of them lies in wait for patience, another strives to violate chastity; that one mocks contempt of the world, this one teaches that one must be zealous for dissimulation. Let us consider these one by one. There is one thought which is born of the prudence of the flesh. This says to a man: Avenge thy hurt, take vengeance for thy injuries, because, if thou bear the present ones patiently, thou wilt embolden others to further ones; let men rather fear thee than despise thee; all will show thee reverence, if they see thee manfully avenging thy injury. See how deceitful, and how false, is that most wicked prophet. He was thought to have persuaded cunningly, when he foretold that reverence would be shown to the raging man; but when he has turned him into rage, he renders him not to be revered but abominable 295Cto all men. There is yet another false prophet, an enemy of integrity and continence, who seduces a man in this order: Often, under the pretext of health or the necessity of work, he urges that fuller and more delicate foods be indulged in; but when he has inflamed the body by surfeit, forthwith he sets the allurements of lust before the eyes; he asserts that it is hard and impossible that a man set in the flesh should not live carnally, that the years of youth can easily be pardoned; that long times still remain for penitence; that God is merciful, that the torments of hell are either none or small. But mark how deceitful this prophet is. He promises delights very many and destined to endure for long times, but a punishment slight and soon to pass away; and perhaps eternal are the 295Dtorments that follow, whereas that which delights cannot stand even for a moment. More deeply, by a certain reason for acting, he teaches that one must be zealous for avarice: that this life is such that a man cannot have honor if he has not means; that riches are often of very great profit not only to bodies but also to the salvation of souls, that from them proceed the sustenance of the poor and the building of churches; lastly, that it is better that he should have whence he may seek merit for himself, than that, having nothing, he be compelled to ask of others, whereby he becomes the more a debtor. But know this prophet also for a liar. He proclaims that for those who possess riches the merits of virtues can be increased, in order that he may take away from those who love divilias all the merits of the virtues, 296Aand may corrupt their minds first by cupidity for seeking riches, then, when they have been acquired, by tenacity for retaining them. There is yet one of the false prophets who urges that one serve dissimulation. For that a good reputation avails as much as may be; that a man ought everywhere to keep his good name unpolluted; and that, if there have been any sins, of infirmity rather than of deliberation, they are to be concealed on account of the scandal of neighbors; that a double loss is incurred if, the one being corrupted by the deed, the other should be corrupted by the example; that therefore a man ought always, for the instruction of others, to display a praiseworthy bearing and one that is a token of virtue, and that he ought with the utmost zeal to avoid that anything sinister or contrary to honor be either said or believed about him; that merit is vehemently 296Bmultiplied if a man both exercise himself by the pursuit of virtue and gain others by his example. O how crafty a promise? He proclaims that merit is to be multiplied through dissimulation, in order that, when he has taught a man both to cover his evils and to make a show of his goods, he may overturn all the solidity of good merit. Concerning these prophets it is said by way of reproach to the sinful soul, set also in tribulation: thy prophets have seen for thee false and foolish things: as if it were said that what the carnal affections promised was false; or, now acknowledge, taught by punishment, what before thou wouldst not attend to, when thou wast being drawn to guilt. False and foolish things have they seen for thee; when by a reason for acting they persuaded thee to evil, they saw false things; but when they drove thee, conquered by cupidity, even to irrational acts, they saw not 296Conly false and foolish things. Whence there follows: Nor did they open to thee thy iniquity. As if it were said: They would open thy iniquity to thee, if, before the deed, they showed what punishment would follow after. But now, that thou mightest not be able to perceive thy iniquity, a feigned reason blinded as it were thy right eye, and depraved delight thy left. Nor did they deceive only by promising prosperity, but also by terrifying through adversities. For they have seen for thee false takings-up, and castings-out. Takings-up, namely, of evils, which they said were hanging over thee, and castings-out of present goods, which they announced were to perish, so that thou, fearing to lose the goods thou didst love and striving to avoid the evils thou didst fear, mightest the more quickly consent to the doing of iniquity.

All they that passed 296Dby the way have clapped their hands over thee; they have hissed, and wagged their heads over the daughter of Jerusalem. LITERAL. As if he should say, hear; and learn, if only by another's testimony, how much thou art to be pitied. By those passing by the way are understood pilgrims and strangers. For those who are named as passing by are shown both to come from elsewhere and to tend elsewhere For by this, that they come from elsewhere, they are shown to be strangers in blood; by this, that they tend elsewhere, strangers in intention. And yet those whom both the affection of blood and the purpose of intention divide, the astonishment at a new calamity fixes in compassion. They have clapped, he says, their hands over thee. Beautifully did he say over thee, as over one cast down and prostrate. They have clapped their hands. There have been some who would reckon that all these things pertain rather to mockery 297Aand derision, because clapping and hissing seem to belong to those who insult. But that in the following clause hissing and insult are treated of again, it is thought to differ in this, that these, as strangers, look down upon lespiciunt her who is cast down, whereas those, as foes and enemies, show by hissing and exulting not only contempt but hatred also. Which sense does not seem to us to run counter to reason either. Yet because, according to the spiritual understanding, these things are more fittingly referred to compassion, we can fittingly say that by the clapping not insult but simply the striking together of the hands is expressed. But the striking together of the hands does not always signify the same thing; rather it indicates sometimes joy, sometimes grief, sometimes wonder, 297Bsometimes compassion. Hissing also indicates diverse affections; for sometimes it hints at contempt, sometimes at despair, sometimes at blandishments. Likewise the wagging of the head expresses sometimes indignation, sometimes insult, sometimes grief, sometimes wonder. But because these motions look to diverse affections, we can understand by the clapping of hands wonder, by hissing despair, by the wagging of the head compassion; or on the contrary, by clapping compassion, and by the wagging of the head wonder. For those passing by, when they behold ruins so lamentable, are provoked to compassion by a certain humanity, and grieve over the present misery which they see; but they despair of the 297Crestoration to come, and marvel at the glory that is past. Out of the grief of compassion they clap their hands; out of despair they hiss with the mouth; out of wonder they wag the head. And this is what follows:

Is this, they say, the city of perfect beauty, the joy of all the earth? As if it were said: Once so glorious, now so wretched. Yet, as has been said, they are to be believed to have said this not in mockery but in compassion.

They have clapped, etc. ALLEGORICAL. First let us consider who these passers-by are, then what is the way through which they pass. This whole present life seems to be a certain passing; since from the moment we enter it by being born, we hasten without intermission through daily changes toward death, as in the psalm 297Dit is read: Man, his days are as grass, as the flower of the field so shall he flourish. For the spirit shall pass through in him, and he shall not stand; and he shall know his place no more (Psal. CII). But there is another passing, a praiseworthy one, which is not common to all. For all men, as we have said, pass through by the necessity of their condition; but the wicked, since by their condition they cannot remain here, nevertheless fix their mind by love upon the desire of this life. The wicked therefore pass by necessity, but by will they do not pass; whereas the good, who do not love the present life but desire the life to come, join will to necessity; nay, to say something truer, they anticipate necessity itself by will, because they depart hence 298Aby will before they do by death. These are the true Hebrews, that is, passers-over: to whom that word of Wisdom is directed: Pass over to me, all ye that desire me (Eccli. XXIV). Of whose number the apostle Paul also glories that he is, when he says: They are Hebrews, so am I (II Cor. XI). For how he is a Hebrew, that is, one passing over, he makes plain in another place. Those things, he says, which are behind being forgotten, I ever stretch myself forth to the things that are before (Philip. III). And again: I have fought the good fight, I have finished the course, I have kept the faith (II Tim. IV). Of this passing also Moses says: I will pass over and see this great vision (Exod. III). Since therefore some pass by necessity alone, while others join to the necessity of their condition the choice of will also, rightly here, when he was speaking of those who pass, did he declare by a necessary addition that he did not take in any and every 298Bpasser-by, saying: Passing by the way. For the way, in sacred discourse, signifies sometimes Christ, sometimes the law of God, sometimes the present life. The way signifies Christ, as he himself testifies, saying: I am the way (Joan. XIV). The way signifies the law of God, as is read in the psalm: Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord (Psal. CXVIII). The way signifies the present life, as is said in the Gospel: Be at agreement with thy adversary whilst thou art in the way (Matth. V). For what is the divine word made to us, unless an adversary, when it sets itself against our depraved wills? To which adversary, namely, we are at agreement in the way, if in this mortal life, where there is still room for meriting, we take pains to obey the precepts of God even against our 298Ccarnal wills. But in this place the way cannot signify the present life, because, since every man passes through it by the necessity of his condition, the prophet would not have made a distinction if, speaking of those who pass, he signified the present life by the way. The way therefore is Christ. And perhaps it is not without cause that, whereas the Psalmist in a certain place has called some men crossers of the way, or those who go beyond it: here he says not those who cross the way, but those who pass through the way. For they cross the way who transgress the law of God, and who either desert the faith of Christ once received, or scorn to receive it when offered through the word of preaching. From these therefore he wished to separate those whom he called not crossers of the way, but passers through the way. And note that he did not say, 298Dstanding in the way; but passing through the way. For they stand in the way who keep a faith idle without good works: who indeed are in the way through right faith, but do not walk through good working. But they pass through the way who, in right faith, by a daily advance in virtues, always tend from good to better. Let it be said therefore: All they that pass by the way have clapped their hands over thee. But what is it that is said to the Church on behalf of her ailing members: All they that pass by the way have clapped their hands over thee; unless that the perils of their neighbors move to compassion all the elect, whom the allurements of this world have no power to hinder from the course of good work or from the desire of eternal things? Which was beautifully signified in the book 299Aof Kings by those two cows which carried the ark of the Lord, returning from the Philistines, set upon a new cart: of which it is written: Taking two cows that gave suck to calves, they yoked them to the cart, and shut up their calves at home (I Reg. VI). And a little after: The cows went straight on by the way which leads to Bethsames, going on and lowing; and they turned not aside, neither to the right hand nor to the left (ibid.). For what do the cows designate, unless all the faithful in the Church; and what the ark, unless the law of God; and what Bethsames, which is interpreted the house of the sun, unless the heavenly country? The cows therefore, as it were carrying the ark set upon them, going on and lowing, go by a straight journey to Bethsames, when the faithful, bearing continually in their heart 299Bthe meditation of the divine law, tend by the way of good work to the heavenly country, and through the nations utter lowings of compassion for those whom carnal affection still binds fast in this world? who neither turn aside from the straight journey on account of compassion, nor cease from the lowing of compassion on account of the purpose of their journey. This same thing the prophet intimates in this place, saying:

All they that pass by the way have clapped their hands over thee. For they clap and at the same time pass by, because they so show the affection of their compassion to the afflicted that yet they neither fail nor turn aside from the purpose of the straight journey. The manner of which compassion is rightly subjoined, when it is said: They have clapped their hands, they have hissed, they have wagged the head. In sacred discourse working is often wont to be designated by the hands, speech by the mouth, and the mind 299Cby the head. If therefore we say that works are designated by the hands, what are we to understand in the clapping of hands, unless the fame and report of good works? For the fame and report of good works sounds in the ears of the people like a certain clapping of hands: and often, when it has resounded suddenly, it turns the eyes of all upon its author, from whom it arose. For hence it is that all the perverse always wish to become known in those things which they seem to do rightly: namely, that, when they have brought their good deeds into the midst to be gazed upon, they may turn the untutored minds of the people to admiration of themselves. But the elect shun to be made manifest in their good acts the more, the more they greatly fear that the reward of eternal recompense is diminished for them by the admiration of earthly praise. And if they sometimes set forth their virtues 299Dand their brave deeds before their neighbors for an example, they by no means do this from a desire of glorying, but from a love of taking counsel for them. Whence here too it is rightly said, under the figure of the desolate city, to the multitude of sinners: All they that pass by the way have clapped their hands over thee. For the elect, who pass by the way of God's commandments from the exile of this world to the heavenly country, often, when they see their neighbors ailing, out of the affection of charity, to rouse their minds, set forth their virtues for an example. But those who pass by a byway do not lament over them that lie fallen; because they display their virtues not for the profit of their neighbors, but for the enlarging of their own glory. There follows: They have hissed, 300Aetc. For the forming of a hiss, the lips are wont to be drawn together on the outside, and within the tongue to be laid under, curved in a manner after the likeness of a channel, so that, when the breath issues drawn through a narrow passage and thinned, it may come forth softer and more soothing to caress the hearing. What therefore can more rightly be understood by the hissing of the mouth than the word of consolation? For to hiss, as it were with the lips drawn together and the tongue laid under, is to bend all the effort of speaking, with words softened and tempered, to the use of consolation. Those therefore who pass by the way both clap their hands and hiss with their mouth over the desolate, when all spiritual men both provoke sinners to do well by the examples of virtues, and confirm them in the hope of pardon by the word of consolation. There follows: They have wagged their head. By the head we can not unfittingly 300Bunderstand the mind, and by the wagging of the head, the compassion of the mind. Rightly therefore do those who pass by, after the clapping and the hissing, also wag the head; because assuredly they cannot effectually console the griefs of others who have not known how truly to suffer with the griefs of others. See whether any of these was lacking to Paul. Paul clapped his hands, when he said: Be ye imitators of me, as I also am of Christ (I Cor. XI). He hissed with his mouth, when consoling the Corinthians after their penitence: I wrote to you not that you should be made sorrowful but that you may know what charity I have more abundantly toward you (II Cor. II). He wagged his head, when he said: My little children, of whom I am in labor again until Christ be formed in you (Galat. IV). There follows in the letter: Over the daughter of Jerusalem. What does Jerusalem, which is interpreted the vision of peace, 300Cdesignate, unless the heavenly country? As if it were said to sinners: You make for any of the elect a grief over your perdition the heavier, the more certain it now is that you ought to have belonged to the fellowship and society of the elect. And just as your salvation would make joy for all, so also from your ruin there proceeds the grief and sadness of all. And this is what follows: Is this, they say, the city of perfect beauty, the joy of all the earth? For the city in this place is to be understood as the catholic Church, in which the gates and the walls are those who both form others by the doctrine of truth and encompass them with watchfulness: which Church, namely, then has perfect beauty when both in the prelates virtue adorns wisdom, and in the subjects obedience adorns the discipline of good works. The beauty therefore of this 300Dcity is rightly called the joy of all the earth; because its discipline is now spread abroad through all parts of the world. Let it be said therefore: Is this the city of perfect beauty, the joy of all the earth? As if it were said: How could she so quickly come into desolation, who formerly by her beauty gladdened all the elect throughout the circuit of the world? But what is it that in the ruin of certain men the whole Church is lamented as though desolate, unless because while one member suffers all the members suffer with it: for which reason they ought greatly to fear who in their own iniquity sadden the charity of others; because assuredly, just as those men by suffering with the evil increase their own merit, so these by saddening the good aggravate their own guilt.

301AThey have clapped, etc. MORAL. He who provokes a sinner to penitence by a gentle exhortation, what else does he do than feel the sick man's wounds before the cutting? As therefore a wound which cannot bear even the gentle touch of remedies is believed to be altogether incurable, so the sinner who spurns even a gentle admonition is reckoned as though incorrigible. This is what the prophet here casts up to the sinful soul with grievous sorrow, saying: They have clapped over thee, etc. As if he were to say: Formerly the suggestion of the flesh could easily prevail with thee to persuade thee to iniquity; but now all the solicitude and industry of good men has failed in thy correction, and as rash as thou wast formerly, through contempt, for perpetrating iniquity, yet now art thou hardened 301Bthrough despair in that very iniquity of thine: All they that pass by the way have clapped their hands over thee. That her ruin may be shown the graver, he recalls that she had already entered upon the way of good work, and had, as it were, collapsed in the middle of the journey. Whence also he calls the others passers-by, that he may plainly show that those who now begin to go before her by the merit of virtues were once behind her. But because in the ruin of their neighbors charity always provokes the elect to compassion, and the example to fear, they are rightly described as both passing by alike and clapping. For by advancing they pass by; by suffering with others they clap, so that they so rejoice over their own advance that yet they do not presume to despise their neighbors lying in infirmity. They have clapped, he says, their hands. The clapping of the hands designates the example of good work; the hissing, the word of consolation; 301Cthe wagging of the head, the affection of compassion; because all spiritual men both provoke by example and confirm by word those whom they see to have fallen through iniquity, and show by the very grief of their own compassion how much they would rejoice over their salvation. There follows: Is this, they say, the city of perfect beauty? They therefore recall what she ought to have been, that from the memory of her past dignity she may the more quickly recognize into what misery she has slipped. They call her a city, as being lofty and fortified with virtues, who formerly had God reigning within her, when she was not yet subjected by consent to the dominion of the vices. There follows: The joy of all the earth. As if it were said: The more there were who ought to have rejoiced over her advance, the more are there now whom her ruin and desolation saddens, so that if 301Dshe will not spare herself, she may at least blush to afflict others by her own perdition. By all these means the hardened conscience is dealt with, that, so manifold a medicine having been applied, it may at length soften to penitence.

All thy enemies have opened their mouth over thee, etc. LITERAL. As if he should say: If it does not move thee that thou hast been mocked by false prophets, that, having fallen into calamity, thou hast been set for a sign and a portent to them that pass by; let this at least prick thy insensibility, that thou hast been made unto thy enemies a prey and a trampling, a joy and a derision. They have opened, he says, their mouth over thee, all thy enemies. The opening of thy mouth designates cruelty and derision. 302AThe greedy have opened the mouth to devour. The proud have opened the mouth to deride.

They have hissed, and gnashed with their teeth. The hissing expresses contempt, the gnashing wrath and indignation. All thy enemies, the Chaldeans, the Romans. Grievous is the pressure where all press and all prevail. There follows:

And they said: We will devour. They wish not to harm a little, but to blot out even to consuming; nor only to blot out, but to devour — they who feed and take delight in thy ruin. Whence there follows:

Lo, this is the day that we have looked for in desiring; we have found it in seeking; we have seen it in exulting. So that the greater is the gladness of those who afflict thee, the more bitter and intolerable is thy misery.

302BThey have opened, etc. ALLEGORICAL. The enemies of holy Church are the heretics, because they assail the constancy of her faith with depraved dogmas. But these do not presume to open their mouth against her, so long as they behold both wisdom and discipline flourishing together in her conduct. For against wisdom they close their mouth, because from those whom they see stable in the knowledge of truth they hide the proofs of their error. Against discipline likewise they close their mouth, when in the conduct of the faithful they do not find what one might blaspheme. But if perchance wisdom should have lost discipline, they open their mouth against her, that they may blaspheme; or if discipline should not have wisdom, they open their mouth against her, that they may disseminate errors; but if neither wisdom nor discipline be in her, now they open their mouth not only against her but 302Cover her, that they may devour, and, as it were by incorporating them into themselves, may consume all the remnants of truth in those whom they are able to draw into their own fellowship There follows: They have hissed. In the hissing we can note the crafty and coaxing persuasions by which they deceive the unwary, and, like serpents after a soft hiss, pour in the venom of death. But because the blandishments of the heretics proceed not from piety but from cruelty: rightly is it subjoined: They have gnashed with their teeth. As if it were said: Coaxing was that which the speech hissed, but exceedingly cruel that which the intention gnashed. There follows: Lo, this is the day that we have looked for, have found, have seen: the oppression of the good they name not night but day, because from that whence the darkness of unbelief blinds others, they themselves shine by an iniquitous gladness. 302DWe have looked for it, they say, namely desiring; we have found it seeking; we have seen it exulting. And note that what is hidden is found; what is seen, is open. As if they should say: What formerly we desired to find, even in secret, now we see made manifest.

They have opened over thee, etc. MORAL. The demons open their mouth against the soul: when they beat with the voices of suggestion upon it as it stands upright in good desires. But they open their mouth above it, when to those lying in depraved delight they no longer persuade iniquity, but command it. Or they open their mouth above her whom, having first drawn her to guilt by coaxing, they afterwards by raging demand for punishment. Whence it is added: They have hissed, 303Athey have gnashed with their teeth. For first they hiss, and then they gnash with their teeth, because, as we have said, after the coaxing suggestion they cruelly demand the punishment. Of which punishment, namely, it is yet further subjoined, when it is said: And they said: We will devour. For what else is it to devour, unless to swallow up unto eternal damnation the soul going forth from the body? But it must be known that they never avail to devour a soul going forth from the body which they have not first, while it was set in the body, boiled down with the fire of the vices. And therefore great confidence is born in them concerning a man's future damnation, when they lord it over him in the present life through iniquity. After therefore he had said, they have opened their mouth, they have hissed, he rightly added at once, saying: They have gnashed with their teeth; they said: We will devour; because from the moment a man is subjected to their suggestion 303Bby consent to sin, they are straightway girded with the fury of the cruelty to come. And because they see the food already softened by long boiling, they promise themselves a devouring close at hand. In which name of devouring, namely, both their vehement desire is shown, and at the same time the bitterness of the damnation is expressed. There follows: Lo, this is the day that we have looked for, have found, have seen. They who say that they have found intimate also that they had sought before they found. First therefore the demons seek, when they take pains to try by suggestion the secret intention of our heart; then they wait, because, although they sometimes receive license to tempt, yet they can never bring force upon the tempted man to consent. But after the search and the waiting 303Cthey find, when, after the suggestion has been sent in, they at last behold manifest signs of a spontaneous consent coming forth from the heart. Last of all they also see, when the consent to the crime proceeds even to the effect of the work.

The Lord hath done that which he purposed, he hath fulfilled his word, etc. LITERAL. Next, after the rebuke, he subjoins consolation, saying: The Lord hath done that which he purposed, etc. First he consoles the mourners by the justice of the judge; then by the malice of the enemy. By the justice of the judge, that they may not grieve over the past; by the malice of the enemy, that they may not despair of the future. For it is a great consolation to the afflicted, that the punishment has been brought upon them by him whom injustice cannot please. Likewise it affords great confidence of obtaining mercy, that 303Dhe is our adversary who is known not to please our judge either. He says therefore: The Lord hath done that which he purposed. As if he were to say: Our enemies exult, and ascribe our ruin to their own strength; but we ought to bear their insult the more lightly, the more truly we know that our evils proceed not from their strength but from the just judgment of God. The Lord hath done that which he purposed. That is shown to have been ordained which is recorded as done with premeditation, especially because it had been foretold long before, so that they may have no occasion of murmuring against God who were able to foreknow the peril and would not beware of it. Whence there follows:

He hath fulfilled his word, which he had commanded from the days 304Aof old. He had commanded his prophets to declare this word, and that they who had been long waited for might at least repent, not newly, but from the days of old, that is, long before it should come to pass. But the longer they were waited for through mercy, the more justly did they, persevering in evil, deserve to be grievously punished. Whence there follows:

He hath destroyed, and hath not spared. These things are properly referred to the last captivity.

And he hath made thine enemy to rejoice over thee. The greater the gladness of the oppressor, the graver is the misery of the oppressed. But yet the arrogance of the enemy is easily despised, if his power be ascribed not to himself but to God. And it is to be noted that I said, over thee he hath made thine enemy to rejoice. For against us, yet not 304Bover us, does the enemy rejoice, when he is indeed permitted to harm but not to oppress; but he rejoices over us when he not only afflicts but also oppresses. There follows:

He hath exalted the horn of thy foes. In the horn strength and power are signified; by the foes, the Chaldeans or the Romans are understood.

The Lord hath done that which he purposed, etc. ALLEGORICAL. Through the heretics the Lord does that which he purposed, because, while they draw away certain men from the Church to their own errors, others become more approved in faith and in the knowledge of truth. Whence there follows: He hath fulfilled his word, which he had commanded from the days of old. For the word of God, which he had commanded from the days of old, is understood to be Holy Scripture, which was long since published to the world; but 304Cis still for a great part hidden. Which is daily fulfilled in the minds of the faithful, when they, exercised by the questions of heretics, grow up to greater pursuits of the virtues, and advance to a loftier understanding of the divine oracles. Thus assuredly do the wicked serve the divine will even then when they strive to go against it, because he so fulfills his disposition through them that by their errors he both permits the reprobate to be ensnared and exercises the good. There follows: He hath destroyed, and hath not spared. When he suffers certain men to be cut off from the unity of the Church through unbelief; he does not spare, because the sorrows of compassion afflict, in the ruin of their brethren, even those who persevere. There follows: He hath made thine enemy to rejoice over thee. The singular enemy of the Church is the devil, who pursues the faithful 304Dwith perpetual hatred, as we read in the Apocalypse of the dragon who persecutes the woman (Apoc. XII). But this enemy then rejoices over holy Church, when he receives even out of her those through whom he afflicts her. There follows: He hath exalted the horn of thy foes. The foes of the Church are the heretics, who daily lead forth a battle-line to storm her. Whose horn is then assuredly said to be exalted, when, many deserting the faith and passing over to their fellowship, the people of the faithful is found lesser in number.

The Lord hath done, etc. MORAL. It is a great mercy of God when those things which a sinner perversely loves are turned to bitterness for him, because by this very thing he is compelled to return to 305Athe love of God, in that he sees set against him those things by desire of which he was being drawn away from the love of God. Hence it is that to the sinful soul, afflicted in her iniquities, this is first brought forward in this place for consolation, namely that her affliction is dispensed at God's nod, so that she may both fear him whose scourges she bears, and at the same time trust in the mercy of him by whom she sees herself visited even through tribulation. The Lord hath done that which he purposed. As if it were said: Because thou wouldst not do what he commanded, he himself has done what he purposed. And because thou didst scorn to fulfill his word which he had commanded thee concerning thy salvation, he himself has fulfilled his word which he had threatened thee concerning thy tribulation. See what was done in the first man. The Lord had commanded him: Of the tree of the knowledge of good 305Band evil eat thou not; he had threatened: In whatsoever day thou shalt eat of it, thou shalt die. The man would not fulfill the command, and God fulfilled the threat. Still daily to those who gape after unlawful and forbidden things God says: Touch not, taste not, in whatsoever day thou shalt eat of it, thou shalt die. I have appointed, I have commanded, that its own punishment should follow every unlawful affection; my threatening can be avoided, but my precept cannot be annulled. From the days of old I commanded this, from the days of old I showed it to be true. For this the first man died, for this the world was blotted out by the water of the flood. Nor in thee will the sentence be able to be changed, which from the beginning of the world down to this time has always remained unchangeable. Rightly therefore is the affliction spoken of: he hath fulfilled the word which 305Che had commanded from the days of old, that she may the more patiently bear the justice of God in her tribulation, in that she sees it exercised upon sinners from the beginning of the world. There follows: He hath destroyed, and hath not spared. What is it to destroy, unless to scatter those things which a man had made ready for unlawful delight? But he destroys and does not spare, when he takes away the delight, and at the same time punishes the guilt by the tribulation that follows. There follows: He hath made thine enemy to rejoice over thee. Our singular enemy is the devil, who rejoices against us when power over those things which cleave to us outwardly is granted him. But when he is permitted to rage even against our very selves, then assuredly he rejoices over us. As we read was done in blessed Job, whose substance he first destroyed, and afterwards struck his flesh. 305DOr the enemy is then said to rejoice over us, when he lords it over us through consent to iniquity. There follows: He hath exalted the horn of thy foes. What do we more rightly understand by the foes of the soul than the desires of the flesh, which assail her with assiduous temptations? Which foes, namely, we then make as it were tributary to us, when we temper the motion of the flesh to the nod of reason. But when the affection of sin begins to hold dominion in us, and the mind follows not the judgment of reason but the appetite of the flesh; then assuredly is the horn, that is, the strength, of our foes exalted. These things God is said to do; because by a just judgment he permits them to be done, so that, while the adversary is known to have been able to do nothing in our guilt save as permitted, he may not be feared even in the punishment. 306AAnd let the human mind the more humbly subject itself to the scourges by the justice of God, the more certainly it has learned by experience that he first long bore with the guilt through patience by himself. For it greatly lightens the weight of tribulation in an afflicted soul, if not even in its own punishment it gainsay contradica the equity of the judge

Their heart cried to the Lord, etc. LITERAL. After the equity of the judge, he consoles the afflicted also by the malice of the enemy; because what God justly permits, that one carries out iniquitously. For while God through him avenges his own injury upon sinners, he himself by an iniquitous intention does injury to God. As is said in Isaiah: Woe to Assur, the rod of my fury, in their hand is my indignation (Isa. X), but he himself does not so judge; of whom here also it is rightly said: Their heart cried to the 306BLord upon the wall of the daughter of Sion. For in the crying of the heart we can understand either the cruelty or the pride of the enemies. Cruelty against those whom they were oppressing; pride against God, whom in the oppression of his people they blasphemed, and believed to be powerless to save. But this thought of theirs cries to God, because in a certain way it provokes him both to have mercy on his own and to punish his adversaries. It cried, he says, upon the wall. They were proud of this, that they had prevailed, that they held the walls and fortifications of the cities. Or by the wall we can understand the divine protection, upon which the enemies cry; because, while they are permitted to hold dominion over the people of God on account of its sins, they detract from the divine power: and they judge that this cannot be hindered 306Cwhich they know not comes to pass by his own permitting and disposing.

It cried, etc. ALLEGORICAL. The wall of the daughter of Sion can signify each several defender of the truth, if through whom the heart of the enemies cries to the Lord; because then do the heretics the more despise, through the elation of their heart, the author of truth, when they turn to their own errors not only the simple in the Church, but the very defenders of the truth themselves.

Their heart cried, etc. MORAL. If by the foes of the soul we understand the desires of the flesh, what are we to understand by the heart of the foes, unless the inmost delight of carnal desires? And again by the wall of the daughter of Sion; because the delight of the flesh is then made injurious to the Creator, when evil desires by the consent 306Dof the mind hold dominion over the virtues. For of God it is said: His place is made in peace (Psal. LXXV); because assuredly God rests sweetly in that mind which, gathering itself from the distraction of this world, composes itself to inward peace. In which, even if outwardly, so far as pertains to the corruption of this life, earthly desires make their din; yet if they are not admitted inward, unto consent, they do not reach as far as the ears of God. But when the delight of the flesh penetrates through consent even to the inner parts of the soul, then assuredly the heart of the foes closed toward God presiding within, so that now he himself, stirred by the importunate din, rising up out of that quiet of his, may say. The cry of the Sodomites and of the Gomorrhaeans is multiplied, and their sin 307Ais grown exceedingly grievous; but I will go down and see whether they have accomplished in deed the cry that is come to me (Gen. XVIII). And rightly; because iniquity is far from God, depraved desires are said to have cried not before God, but to God. For praise and thanksgiving cry not only to God, but also before God. Necessity and iniquity cry to God. For when we praise the power and magnificence of God, or when we give him thanks for the mercy bestowed upon us, then assuredly we cry before God. But when by acting iniquitously we provoke him to wrath, or when, set in necessity, we demand his help, then we cry to him as it were from afar. Iniquity therefore cries to God, and necessity cries; but the one cries provoking, 307Bthe other beseeching; the one calls forth wrath, the other begs mercy. Of the cry of necessity it is said: The cry of the children of Israel is come to me (Exod. III), and I have seen their affliction, wherewith they are oppressed by the Egyptians. Of the cry of iniquity it is said: The cry of the Sodomites and of the Gomorrhaeans is multiplied, and their sin is grown exceedingly grievous. Of the cry of praise it is said: They shall cry out, yea they shall sing a hymn (Psal. LXIV). Of the cry of devotion it is said: My cry before him entered into his ears (Psal. XVII). The angels cry in heaven with the cry of praise; Moses cries in the desert with the cry of devotion; the children of Israel cry in Egypt with the cry of necessity; Sodom and Gomorrha cry with the cry of iniquity. But then is the cry of iniquity said to reach 307CGod, when malice grows so great that it deserves to be borne no longer.

Bring down thy tears like a torrent by day and by night. LITERAL. Their minds having been raised up by the consolation, an exhortation follows, in which sinners are invited to penitence. Nor is it without cause that thus, as it were abruptly, in the middle of a clause, he suddenly seizes upon another matter. But because in the very course of the discourse an occasion of exhorting offered itself, and what is subjoined is wont the more to move minds, inferring it as it were from the preceding, he thus says: Bring down thy tears like a torrent, as if he were to say: Because thine enemies provoke God to wrath through elation, do thou implore his mercy through the humility of penitence; and by the 307Dtorrent is signified the vehement motion of compunction. The perseverance of which is also subjoined, when it is said: By day and by night. Insistence too is added, when it is subjoined: Give thyself no rest. As if it were said: Neither intermit nor slacken the rush of tears, but at every time and with all insistence press on with weeping; because the tears themselves also have their own voice, and will cry for thee to the Lord. This is what follows:

Neither let the apple of thine eye be silent. That is a word of mercy. The apple, he says — let the storm of tears bursting in darken not only the rim of the eyes, but the very ray of vision itself, so that, while even that which is tender is afflicted, the tenderness of the judge may the more quickly be moved to mercy.

308ABring down thy tears like a torrent. ALLEGORICAL. The prophetic discourse exhorts holy Church to offer tears to the Lord for her ailing members. Which tears he compares to a torrent. For a torrent is a stream gathered out of winter waters, which indeed runs down with a rush, but quickly dries up and passes away. And what is the present life, unless a winter, in which, before the brightness of eternal life appears, the clouds of error still enfold the hearts of men? Rightly therefore is holy Church commanded to bring down tears like a torrent, because, namely, all the elect who gape after the joys of the heavenly country sigh without ceasing over the misery of the present life. But then without doubt will this torrent of tears be dried up by the heat of the summer to come, when, the eternal brightness shining forth, 308Ball grief will be turned into joy, so that there can then be neither any grief over those who are lost, nor fear over those who are to perish. There follows: By day and by night. By the day prosperity is signified, by the night adversity. Because therefore holy Church beholds some deceived by the prosperity of this life, some broken by adversity, lamenting the ruin of both she brings forth tears as it were by day and by night. There follows: Give thyself no rest. All the elect, while they lament others, give no rest to themselves either; because while they behold the falls of others, they become the more solicitous about their own salvation. There follows: Neither let the apple of thine eye be silent. The apple of the eye, by which the sight is directed, fittingly designates all spiritual men in holy Church, by whose word and example the whole body of the Church is enlightened. Who 308Cassuredly ought by no means to be silent in the peril of their neighbors, but, as far as they can, both to relieve them with prayers and to raise them up with preaching. For because they can better foresee their peril than they themselves can, they truly sin if they are not even more solicitous about their salvation than they themselves are. Which that singular apple of the eye, through whom all are enlightened, that is, the Lord Jesus Christ, well showed, when, foreseeing the ruin of the faithless city, he wept while she herself exulted, and, set in his passion and praying for his persecutors, was solicitous about their salvation. The apple of the eye therefore ought not to have silent tears; because all spiritual men must, with the affection of compassion, also show forth the word of preaching.

308DLead down thy tears like a torrent. MORAL. Here a perfect form of repenting is set forth. For true repentance begins from compunction, which by the water of tears washes away also the filth of sins, and by watering the soul makes it fruitful for the shoots of the virtues. Whence it is said to the sinful soul that she first lead down her tears, that is, lead them downward. For we lead the streams of tears upward, as it were, when we sigh with longing for the heavenly fatherland. But when, pricked with compunction at the remembrance of our sins, we weep, we lead the streams of our tears down to the lower places. Yet it comes first that each man wash away the stains of his sins in the fountain of tears, and then, his conscience cleansed, turn the channel of his compunction to the love of things eternal. In this too, that the sinner is bidden to lead his tears 309Adownward, humility together with compunction is shown to be necessary for the penitent, according to the saying of the Psalmist, who says: A contrite and humbled heart, O God, thou wilt not despise (Psal. I). We may note something yet more subtle in the term "leading down." For what is led is indeed moved of itself, and yet its motion is directed by another's will. But there are some who, when they have seen others weeping, blush to have dry eyes, and wringing out tears by a kind of violence, deceive the eyes of men with a feigned compunction. These, assuredly, we more rightly say to drag tears out than to lead them down. There are likewise others who, through impatience of heart at some trouble befalling from without, pour forth tears — tears which nevertheless they by no means themselves lead out, 309Bsince they do not go before with reason tears that flow of their own accord from the motion of the heart. But those who truly repent do lead out their tears, because, as these rise of themselves from the very fountain of compunction, they direct them with reason going before, so that they neither burst forth through impatience, nor spread themselves too loosely out of the secret of their own channel into the open field of human praise. But in what manner tears are to be led out is beautifully suggested when it is said: Lead down thy tears like a torrent. For a torrent runs down with great force; but the more violently it flows away, the more quickly it passes by. Rightly therefore is the fervor of compunction compared to a torrent, because the greater the force of grief has been in the compunction, the more quickly it passes by, divine consolation coming upon it. But because to begin a good thing profits little unless it be held 309Cperseveringly, rightly he added what follows: By day and night. For there are some who after their tears dissolve the mind in empty gladness, and because they neglect to hold their heart perseveringly in mourning, they lose whatever they had been able to obtain in the time of compunction. For a sinful conscience, just as before the ardor of compunction it is often afflicted with a heavy weariness of mourning at the remembrance of its sins, so sometimes after the consolation of tears, if a diligent strictness does not keep it down, is scattered abroad through unfitting gladness. And therefore it is exceedingly necessary that, after the grace of compunction has raised the mind up out of mourning, lest afterward its own softness dissolve it through immoderate gladness, the guard of the former 309Dmourning never leave it. Rightly therefore is the sinner admonished to lead down his tears by day and night. For the weariness of mourning is, as it were, a kind of night of the mind; but the day of the mind is the joy of compunction. As it were by night, then, he leads down his tears who in the weariness of mourning lifts up his spirit through compunction. By day he leads down his tears who after the gladness of compunction keeps his mind in mourning. Let it therefore be said to the penitent: Lead down thy tears like a torrent by day and night. As if it were said: Thou who considerest that thou hast sinned much, it is needful that thou spare thyself neither in the greatness nor in the length of thy grief. But because many do not indeed forsake the zeal of repenting once begun, yet nevertheless grow lukewarm little by little, as the time comes on, from that fervor of theirs with which they had begun. He adds, 310Asaying: Give thyself no rest, because likewise the devotion of humility renders the persistence of labor commendable to God. Lastly he subjoins and says: Neither let the apple of thine eye be silent. For what ought we to understand by the apple of the eye but the humble devotion of the mind? When therefore no rest is given, the apple of the eye also cries out: if, while the flesh is afflicted without, within the devotion of the mind prays.

Arise, give praise in the night, in the beginning of the watches. LITERAL. As far as concerns the letter, there are three watches of the night, each marked off by the space of three hours. And by a wonderful dispensation enough the prophet, inviting sinners to repentance, does not at once set forth the very perfection of repenting, but advances his exhortation by little and little, so that what perhaps a weak mind 310Bcould not take in all at once it may more easily bear when divided. Above he had enjoined sinners to pour forth tears in repentance for their sins; here he now enjoins that in their tribulation they praise also the justice of God; there he bids them weep in the night, here even to rise up to weeping in the beginning of the watches. It is more, then, to rise up to weeping than only to weep; more in the beginning of the watches than in the night. There follows:

Pour out thy heart like water. He pours out his heart like water who brings forth tears from the inmost affection of the heart; and suggesting this yet more plainly, he adds:

Lift up thy hands to him. For the lifting up of hands expresses the affection of the heart. Or certainly those who lift up their hands show that they are in need and desire help. And therefore rightly does the people set in tribulation 310Clift its hands to him, that it may show that it has hope not in its own strength, but in the help of God alone. There follows:

For the soul, that is, for the life, of thy little ones: who perish with hunger at the head of all the crossways. The heads of the crossways are where several roads coming from different quarters run together into one. Which places are wont to be more frequented by men, so that the greater shame of those wasting away there may be suggested. And see how it is set in a narrow strait: where the case at issue is of obtaining life, when parents are bidden to entreat for the soul of their little ones. And therefore parents are bidden to entreat for the soul of their little ones; and therefore parents ought to make supplication for their little ones, 310Dbecause for the fault of the parents the little ones bear the punishment, so that the remedy may come from that quarter whence the peril came.

Arise, give praise in the night, etc. ALLEGORICAL. What is signified by the night but the life of sinners? Such as those once were to whom Paul speaks, saying: You were once darkness, but now light in the Lord (Ephes. V). The prophetic word therefore admonishes holy Church to arise and give praise in the night. For she arises when, for the salvation of her neighbors whom she sees to be in peril, she raises herself from the quiet of contemplation to the work of preaching. She gives praise in the night when, calling sinners to repentance, she makes manifest to them the mighty works of God. But there are many sinners who even scorn to hear the word of truth, and who so much the more do not incline the ear of the heart to the praise of the Creator as they are the less awake in considering their own sins. 311AOf whom it is read in the psalm: On the willows in the midst thereof we hung up our instruments (Psal. CXXXVI). And in the Gospel: Cast not your pearls before swine (Matth. VII). And therefore, when he had said: Give praise in the night, rightly he added what follows, in the beginning of the watches. As if he were saying: Those sinners are able to hear the praise of God who have begun to keep watch in considering their own sins. There follows:

Pour out thy heart like water. What is figured by the heart but deep understanding? and what by water but easy teaching? He therefore pours out his heart like water who suggests to weak hearers, by an easy instruction without, that which he tastes as solid within. As the Psalmist says: Day unto day uttereth speech, 311Band night unto night sheweth knowledge (Psal. XVIII). But he who for the love of God willingly condescends to weak hearers, he beyond doubt pours out his heart like water before the Lord. There follows:

Lift up thy hands to him for the soul of thy little ones. By the lifting up of hands we may understand the affection of the heart; by the little ones, those weak in faith and not yet having a mature understanding. Holy Church therefore lifts up her hands to God for the soul of her little ones, because for the salvation of those who are as yet small in faith she not only exercises herself in the word of preaching, but also with humble devotion continually entreats the divine mercy. Or by the hands we may understand works, because he assuredly lifts up his hands to God for the soul of the little ones, because, pressing on with spiritual pursuits to rouse 311Cthe minds of carnal men, he avoids even certain lawful occupations of human intercourse. As Paul did, who, that he might take away from the false apostles their occasion of receiving, altogether refused to accept the stipends of preaching — which by custom, nay rather by divine institution, it was lawful to accept — seeking his living with his own hands. Concerning which little ones it is further subjoined:

Who have fainted with hunger at the head of all the crossways. What are we to understand by the crossways but human actions? and what by the head of the crossways but the concupiscence of the flesh, which is the cause and origin of all human actions? The little ones therefore faint with hunger at the head of the crossways, because all carnal men, 311Dthe more they gape after earthly desires, so much the more do they fast from the pasture of spiritual nourishment.

Arise, give praise. MORAL. A certain new life seems here to rise upon me. Arise, give praise. How beautiful an order: first to wipe away past evils with weeping, afterward to rise up to the doing of good things, and then to give praise? For praise is not comely in the mouth of a sinner (Eccli. XV); nor can he truly do good things who has not first striven effectually to amend the evils that are past. Give praise, he says, in the night, in the beginning of the watches. A sinner set under the scourge offers a sacrifice pleasing to God if he praises God even out of his own tribulation. Whence rightly, when the prophet had said, give praise in the night — that is, in tribulation — he at once added: In the beginning of the watches. For a sinner first keeps watch when 312Ahe begins to acknowledge his own evils. He keeps watch a second time when he corrects by repentance the evils he has acknowledged. He keeps watch a third time when he turns himself to the doing of good things. He keeps watch a fourth time when he guards his good works. These four watches we ought to keep in the night of the present life: that is, that ignorance may not deceive us in the consideration of our sins, nor negligence or contempt hinder us in their correction; that sloth may not render us sluggish for the doing of good things, nor presumption make us less wary for the keeping of them. He therefore praises God in the night, in the beginning of the watches, who confesses that the evils he suffers he endures by the just judgment of God, out of the consideration of his own iniquity. There follows: Pour out thy heart like water before the face of the Lord. Other liquids, when they are poured out, 312Bleave the vessels either smeared over with a kind of greasiness or tainted with a savor; water alone is so poured out that the cleanness of the vessel is stained by no remnants of it. Those, therefore, who in confessing their sins are afraid to pour out some of them, whether by reason of negligence or of shame, retain, as it were, in the vessel of the heart the remnants of a thick liquid. But those who do indeed cast out all their sins by confession, and yet still do not forsake the affection for sinning — from these, though the harmful liquid has been wholly cast out, the vessels have nevertheless remained tainted with the savor. But whoever, by a pure confession and contrition revealing the secrets of his heart, retains nothing by affection nor covers anything by silence, he pours out his heart like waters before the face of the Lord. We may yet in the pouring out of water 312Cattend to something of edification, because as water poured out washes away filth, so confession, casting out sins, renders the conscience clean. And well is the heart said to be poured out like water before the face of the Lord, because unless a man shut out from his heart, along with the evil deed, the affection also for sinning, he is not fully excused before God, his conscience bearing witness. There follows: Lift up thy hands to him. By the hands is signified working. The penitent therefore lifts up his hands to God when by the showing forth of good works he excuses his past offenses before God. Whereof it is further subjoined: For the soul of thy little ones, who have fainted with hunger at the head of all the crossways. By the little ones we not unfittingly understand the weak thoughts of the soul; by the head of all 312Dthe crossways, the concupiscence of the flesh, which is, as it were, the head and origin of all earthly desires and actions, as of many roads. At the head of the crossways, then, our little ones faint with hunger, because our tender thoughts, while they cleave to carnal desires, lose the pasture of spiritual nourishment. But we lift up our hands to God for the soul of our little ones when, through zeal for good works, we beg that the grace of spiritual desire be renewed in us for the nourishing of the soul's thoughts. And these our little ones do indeed faint with hunger at the head of the crossways, because the tender thoughts of the mind, which good desires do not feed within, being soon softened by the concupiscence of the flesh (which is the head of all evil desires), lose all the strength 313Aof virtue. Or the concupiscence itself is the hunger in which they faint with hunger, because they always hunger and cannot be filled.

See, O Lord, and consider whom thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. LITERAL. This man does not desist from his purpose until he brings what he has begun to its end. For hitherto he has dealt with sinners, that he might bend them to repentance; now, taking up the cause of the penitent, he turns his speech to God, that he may entreat him on behalf of sinners. And at the same time he gives to penitents a form of praying, plainly showing how greatly a sinner ought to humble himself in his prayers, since he himself, praying for the sins of others, prostrates himself with so humble a devotion before the divine goodness. For because he considers that he has taken up a difficult cause, he by no means presumes to knock at the ears of the divine 313Bmajesty with an open demand; but with great reverence and trembling he shows the affection of his petition by suggestion, saying: See, O Lord, and consider whom thou hast gleaned videmiaveris thus. As if he were saying: Thy people, whom thou hast so greatly loved, who had no other protector beside thee alone — dost thou so gather them as a vintage, so oppress them, so lay them waste? Consider, I pray, our present calamity; call back to memory thy former love; that if thou art not bent by our prayers, thou mayest at least be moved to compassion and mercy by thine own benefits. Thou hast gathered as a vintage, he says. That people, then, was not a woodland, but a vineyard having a keeper and bearing fruit. And yet the wood of the vine, without cultivation and fruit, is useless, and cheaper than all other woods. Why then dost thou lay waste him whom by tending thou makest precious, and by laying waste 313Ccheap and useless? Whom thou hast gathered as a vintage, he says, thus. How thus? So horribly, so monstrously, that the vineyard itself devours its own clusters, and women eat their own sons. Therefore he cries out and says:

Shall the women then, O Lord, eat their own fruit. That is, their own sons, little ones namely of a span's measure, that is, tender ones, scarcely yet having the measure of a span; so that a monstrous cruelty, nay rather a cruel necessity, may be shown. And well does he say women, not mothers: because they are not to be called by the name of mother who were not mindful of a mother's love. Their own fruit, he says, they shall eat. As if he were saying: Other growing things bear fruit for this reason, that the nature of the kind, 313Dwhich is consumed in the parent, may be repaired in the offspring of its own seed; but man, forgetful of his own nature, consumes the very fruit by which he ought to have been repaired. But lest by thus magnifying the misery he should seem not to be entreating God but provoking him, and as it were charging with injustice one who had so cruelly punished his own people, after the punishment has been recalled he subjoins the fault also, using a certain shrewd concession — namely, that while in confessing the fault he does not differ from the justice of God, in seeking mercy he may the more quickly bring his petition to effect, saying:

If the priest and the prophet be slain in the sanctuary of the Lord. As if he were saying: Because they slew thy prophets and thy priests in thy sanctuary — as is read of Zacharias the son of Joiada the priest, whom Joas 314Athe king killed: because, I say, they slew them, therefore the women ate the fruit of their own womb, as is read of Mary the daughter of Eleazar; and it is written in the book of Kings (II Par. XXVI), and Josephus also bears witness to it. But in that he says, if he be slain, it is as though it were said: Although they had sinned so greatly, ought they nevertheless to have been punished with so monstrous a vengeance? Yet in this he does not reprehend the judgment of God, but conceding the fault and aggravating the punishment, he seeks mercy. As if he were saying: There is no people whom thou hast so greatly loved, on whom thou hast bestowed so great benefits; there is no people that has so greatly sinned against thee; there is no people whom thou hast so greatly cast down, so greatly gathered as a vintage and destroyed: and therefore, I pray, see — not in passing, but consider diligently — our misery, 314Bthat thou mayest again bestow upon us thy wonted mercy.

See, O Lord, and consider whom thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. ALLEGORICAL. That holy Church is called a vineyard is proved by very many testimonies of the Scriptures. Among which is that word of the Gospel, where the householder is said to have sent laborers into his vineyard (Matth. XX): because, namely, God the Father, when he sends preachers to instruct the Church, sends laborers as it were to cultivate his vineyard. For just as the vine is cultivated with great zeal indeed and labor, so assuredly the life of the faithful is formed to virtue only with great zeal and labor. And just as a vineyard, if it has been fruitful, makes good by the abundance of its fruits whatever labor is spent upon it, so assuredly 314Cthe life of the faithful, if by the zeal of discipline and instruction it has advanced to virtue, repays with precious fruit all the diligence bestowed upon it. Again, as the wood of the vine without fruit is useless and cheaper than all other woods, so surely those who are cultivated by the teaching of faith and yet do not bring forth the fruit of good work are worse than those who have never come to the knowledge of the truth. And it sometimes happens that God, withdrawing his grace, forsakes such men so much the more deeply as he had the longer borne with them, ungrateful after grace bestowed. Yet the fall of these all perfect men bewail out of the compassion of charity; and they who rejoiced that these were already through faith members with them of the same body cannot bear their cutting off without grief. 314DIt is in their voice that this is here said: See, O Lord, and consider whom thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. God is said to gather the wicked as a vintage when those whom he himself first cultivated through his preachers he forsakes, his grace withdrawn, barren and empty. The vintaging itself can also be referred to the elect: because assuredly, when certain men are withdrawn through unbelief from the unity of the Church, holy Church herself seems to have been left bare and as it were despoiled. Whence with great affection of devotion this must be pronounced which is said: See, O Lord, and consider whom — namely, thy people, thy little flock — thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. How? He subjoins: Shall the women then eat their own fruit, little ones of a span's measure? By the women we rightly understand evil prelates, softened with a carnal understanding and loose 315Amorals: who eat their own fruit, because they receive in the present life the reward of the labor of their ministry. To good teachers, on the contrary, it was said: I have appointed you that you should go and should bring forth fruit, and your fruit should remain (Joan. XV). For the fruit of the elect is said to remain, because in them there is kept for their labor no passing wages, but an eternal reward. The women therefore eat their own fruit, because all carnal men, while in this life they receive the wages of their labor, do not, as it were, keep their fruit, but consume it. But what this fruit is, is declared when it is said: Little ones of a span's measure. Here we ought to understand the little ones not in respect of age, but of faith. And by the span we not unfittingly understand the beginning of a good work. What is it then that the fruit 315Bof the women is called little ones, but that the faith of believers is reckoned to their teachers for a reward? And these carnal prelates eat: because assuredly, while they gape after earthly things, they drag down to destruction, by the example of their evil work, the weak in faith and the beginners whom they had first begotten in faith through the word of preaching. Or certainly the women then eat their own little ones when evil prelates in the Church afflict with want those subjects whom they ought to have fed with the food of heavenly nourishment, bitterly despoiling them of their goods. There follows: If the priest and the prophet be slain in the sanctuary of the Lord? By the priest and the prophet we ought here to understand the same men whom a little before we understood to be signified by the women: namely, prelates, who are both prophets by the word and priests by the ministry. 315CAnd by the sanctuary is expressed divine worship: as if it were said: If prelates perish by administering spiritual things unworthily, why then do their crimes entangle their innocent subjects?

See, O Lord, and consider whom thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. MORAL. By a fitting likeness the soul is said to be gathered as a vintage by God when, its own evil deserts requiring it, it is despoiled of the gifts of the virtues by the just judgment of God. But there is a certain vintaging more destructive than the rest. For it is less perilous to lose virtue than to be proud of virtue. For he who loses virtue at least acknowledges his own loss; but he who is proud of virtue bears a loss and does not know it. The prophet, bewailing a soul not only sinful but also emptily glorying in a false virtue — See, he says, O Lord, 315Dand consider whom thou hast gathered thus as a vintage. And as though asking, How thus? he subjoins with a cry and says: Shall the women then eat their own fruit? For the women eat their own fruit when carnal souls feed themselves through empty glory upon the work of virtue. They eat the fruit, because the virtue itself is consumed from that very thing wherein the mind is delighted with an unlawful refreshment. Their own fruit, he says. What manner of fruit? Little ones of a span's measure. By the little ones we may understand tender virtues, not yet advanced to a manly strength; but by the span, the beginning of a good work. Little ones of a span's measure, then, are tender virtues, newly brought forth at the beginning of good working. What is it then that the women are said to eat little ones of a 316Aspan's measure, but that carnal minds glory even in the beginning of a good work? There follows: If the priest and the prophet be slain in the sanctuary of the Lord? The mind of a just man, which is the sanctuary of God, ought to have two things in itself: namely prudence and devotion — prudence, whereby it may trample down impending evils by foreseeing them; devotion, whereby it may wipe away past evils by supplication. What then do we understand by devotion but a kind of priest of the soul continually offering the sacrifice of expiation in the sight of the Lord; and what by prudence but a prophet? Because therefore the priest and the prophet is slain in the sanctuary of the Lord, the women eat their own little ones: for never would the mind of man exalt itself over its own virtue, unless first, through negligence, both the memory of praeteritotorum 316Bevils and the wariness of things to come had grown lukewarm within it.

The child and the old men [senex] lay on the ground of fury: my virgins and my young men are fallen by the sword. LITERAL. Or it is to be distinguished thus: The child and the old man lay on the ground, the virgins and the young men fell by the sword. Or thus: The child and the old man and the virgins lay on the ground, and the young men fell by the sword. Which perhaps agrees better for this reason: because virgins are not wont to be slain by the sword in war, but to be led away for plunder and spoil — unless by this very thing the fierceness of the enemy be marked out, who spared not even virgins. He says, then, that the warrior people fell by the sword together with their kings and princes, and that the rest of the multitude, of either sex and every age, was dragged away into captivity. 316CThe child, he says, and the old man, and the virgins — the weak age, the weak sex — lay on the ground, cast down without mercy, without humanity, on the ground of fury. Not only on the ground, which of itself might suffice for such a punishment, but on the ground of fury. Either in their own land, which the Lord in his fury permitted to be laid waste and depopulated; or in a foreign land, to which the fury of the Lord drove them out. There follows:

Thou hast slain in the day of fury, thou hast struck, and thou hast no mercy [misertus es ]. As if he should say: To thee, O Lord, do we ascribe our calamity; concerning our misery we enter into a cansam with thee. For the blow which the fury of the raging enemy inflicted, thy wrath, provoked first by our sins, dictated. Thou hast slain those who fell by the sword; thou hast struck those who lay on the ground; and thou hast 316Dno mercy, in that, when thou seest so many already to have perished, thou dost not turn away the scourge of thy indignation at least from those who yet survive. I beseech thee, O Lord, I entreat thee, O Lord, have mercy: that the destruction into which we fell while thou wast angry, we may escape while thou art merciful.

The child and the old men lay on the ground. ALLEGORICAL. As the people, so also the priests. They lay on the ground: they took their rest in earthly desires. And not only did they rest (which was signified above, when it was said: The old men sat on the ground, they held their peace), but they also slept: for they lay down, so that now they know not their own selves; and, all reason being lulled asleep, they consider not whence they came or whither they tend. To whom it is said by Paul: Arise, thou that sleepest: and rise up from the dead, and 317Ashall enlighten thee illuminabi te Christ (Ephes. V). Alas! how ill do they lie who lie on the ground! how ill do they sleep who sleep on the ground! who take their rest on the ground, and when they awake find nothing of riches in their hands! For whatever is loved in time is like a dream, and vanishes away when a man has begun to awake in death. On the ground, he says, of fury they lay. What ought we to understand by the ground of fury but this world, in which the human race, for the punishment of the first guilt, was cast out from the happiness of paradise and condemned to exile? How great a madness, then, that a man should seek rest in this pilgrimage, into which he doubts not that he has been cast in the wrath of divine vengeance? There follows: My virgins and my young men are fallen by the sword. By the sword 317Bin this place is signified the teaching of heretics, of whom it is said in the psalm: Their tongue is a sword aculus (Psal. LVI); because, while they drag some away from the unity of the catholic faith to their own errors, they cut off, as it were, members from the body. And by the virgins and the young men the faithful are signified, who are both virgins, because they corrupt the integrity of faith by depraved works; and are young men, because through constancy of mind they do not yield when adversities press upon them. What is it then that virgins are said to fall by the sword, but that — the Apostle bearing witness — evil communications corrupt good manners (I Cor. XV), and the doctrines of heretics, in which they prevail to violate the chastity of faith, afterward overthrow also the integrity of good works? Young men fall by the sword when those whose constancy could not be 317Covercome by adversities, cut down by the guiles of words, rush headlong into unbelief. Or if we follow the other distinction, the virgins lie on the ground when those men gape in mind after earthly desires who outwardly, by the showing forth of their work, feign a chastity of intention. By the young men, too, we may understand the proud and those presuming upon their own strength, because the life of man flourishes chiefly at this age both in heat and in vigor. Young men therefore fall by the sword, because since thou, O God, dost despise the proud, it is plain of those who are overthrown by the errors of heretics that they would by no means fall away from the knowledge of the truth had they not first been high-minded concerning themselves. There follows: Thou hast slain in the day of fury; thou hast struck, and thou hast no mercy. He slew those who fell headlong through unbelief; 317Dhe struck those who in the fall of the perishing were wounded through the suffering of charity. He has no mercy, because while he does not raise up the fallen, neither does he console the grief of those who stand.

The child and the old men lay on the ground of fury. MORAL. The ground of fury is our mortal flesh, which before the sin of the first man brought forth the shoots of the virtues; but afterward, by the sentence of the curse, began to bring forth the thorns and thistles of the vices But the little child is the sense of the flesh, because, so far as in it lies, it always seeks after certain childish and empty things. And as by the little child we understand the sense of the flesh, so by the old men we may not unfittingly understand the judgment and the appetite of the mind; because the mind ought both to be mature in discerning and not rash in desiring. 318AThe little child therefore lies with the old men on the ground of fury when that very portion of our corruption which the punishment of the transgression brought in, the carnal mind spreads beneath itself for its delight not only according to sense but also according to judgment and appetite, and thinks to take its rest there where it has been cast down to be beaten. There follows: My virgins and my young men are fallen by the sword.
