By the virgins we may not unfittingly understand chaste thoughts; by the young men, the fortitude and constancy of the soul; by the sword, the temptation of the adversary. For because the age of youth flourishes with fuller vigor, what else are the fortitude of doing well and the constancy of persevering but a kind of young men in the rational soul? For they are both prompt to begin a work, and persist without failing 318Bin the work begun. Rightly therefore are the old men said first to have lain on the ground, and afterward the virgins and the young men are recorded to have fallen by the sword; because from the moment that the judgment and appetite of the mind is corrupted by earthly delight, a temptation coming upon it easily both violates the integrity of good thoughts and unstrings the fortitude and constancy of the soul. For because, as has often been said, thoughts proceed from affections, while thoughts are corrupted from corrupt ones, they draw bitterness as it were from an infected root. And when the carnal soul desires or discerns nothing outside the flesh, all its fortitude and constancy soon grows soft in the temptation of the flesh. Nor is this to be passed over, that when the old men sit on the ground, the virgins sprinkle their heads with ashes; when the old men are clothed 318Cwith haircloth, the virgins cast their heads down to the ground; when the old men lie on the ground, the virgins fall by the sword — because assuredly the corruption of thoughts grows according to the corruption of affections. There follows: Thou hast slain in the day of fury, thou hast struck, and thou hast no mercy. God sometimes permits certain virtues in the soul to be destroyed by temptation, and certain others to be wounded: so that while the tempted man sees that he has kept some good things still, he may not fall utterly into despair; and while he sees that he has lost certain things, he may not presume upon his own strength, and may the more truly recover what he has lost the more subtly, taught by his own loss, he now sees that the very thing he has kept is not of himself. But because many are permitted to be tempted not for their correction but for their overthrow, 318Dtherefore a man, who cannot always know the cause of his own temptation, ought always to fear peril; and on this account it is rightly subjoined in the voice of those who tremble: And thou hast no mercy. Which sentence assuredly is one of fear, not of assertion. For because he does not quickly deliver from temptation, the tempted man therefore thinks his scourge to be of wrath and not of mercy; which nevertheless belongs not to one despairing but to one fearing, as we have said.

Thou hast called as to a solemn day. LITERAL. As if he should say: Enemies indeed are always ready to do harm; but nevertheless, unless they are called, they cannot come. This man therefore does not accuse God but honors him, who attributes nothing to the strength of enemies, but extols the divine power even in his own oppression. Thou hast called, 319Ahe says. As if he were saying: Theirs it was to will, thine to grant; that from this very thing thou mayest consider that it is thine also to deliver. Thou hast called as to a solemn day, that is, to a day of gladness and joy: not that thy goodness was delighted in our misery, but because their malice exulted in this, so that this very thing may provoke thee to have mercy, and thou mayest more quickly have compassion on us, while thou beholdest them wickedly rejoicing over us.

Those that should terrify me round about. Those who should hem me in on every side, that I might not escape the hands of my pursuers, after the likeness of hunters who by shouting drive wild beasts into the nets. He marks that time when, as the Jews were fleeing the Babylonians, the neighboring nations on every side blocked against them all defense of flight, that they might not escape the hands of their enemies when Sedecias, fleeing with an army 319Bof warriors, was taken in the plain of Jericho, and brought back to Reblata to the king of Babylon, and there, his sons having been slain before him, was himself blinded and carried away to Babylon. Whence there follows:

And there was none in the day of the fury of the Lord that escaped and was left. Was left — that is, understood, alive; or was left in his own land, and not carried away captive.

Whom I brought up and nourished. I brought up unto discipline, and I nourished unto strength: who are the more to be bewailed.

The enemy hath consumed them. That is, he has blotted them out even to utter destruction.

Thou hast called as to a solemn day: those that should terrify me round about. ALLEGORICAL. It is not unfitting that 319Cin this place the time of the last examination should be signified: which day is called solemn either for this reason, that then the joys of the just shall begin, or because then all things shall be brought into the midst to be sifted. Which time assuredly no one awaits without terror, just as there is no one to whom it falls to pass through the course of the present life without fault. But the elect, while they afflict themselves with lamentations for the misery of the present life, also bring before the eyes of the mind the evils that remain; and by anxiously considering the terrors to come, they judge themselves here, that they may escape judgment there. They consider the most savage exactors, the demons, at whose presence even the elect shall be terrified: who shall press hard on every side upon those who are to be judged, so that no one would have room to escape, did not the mercy of the judge 319Dtemper the strictness of vengeance. When the enemy shall consume many who, brought up here in the discipline of the Church and nourished in the teaching of truth, seemed to be reckoned among the sons of God, and shall drag them with him to perdition. It can also be said of pagans and heretics, or of any unbelievers whatsoever, and chiefly in the time of Antichrist, when the devil shall be let loose on every side in his own members for the persecution of the faithful. Which day is called solemn for this reason, that then the wicked shall rejoice over the oppression of the good; because there is none that escapes and is left, while some who persist in the truth they punish bodily, and others who consent to them they slay spiritually. Whence there follows: Whom I brought up and nourished, the enemy hath consumed; because assuredly many shall then fall headlong into unbelief 320Awho were brought up in the discipline of the Church and nourished in her teaching. I brought up, he says, and I nourished — not only nourished, because the enemy has consumed even the strong, whom the weight of tribulation has overwhelmed. The enemy can indeed afflict the good by persecuting them, but he cannot consume them; but he who is overcome by tribulation, he is consumed by the enemy.

Thou hast called as to a solemn day, those that should terrify me round about. Human life is, as it were, a kind of circuit, which after the running out of temporal actions returns at last through death thither whence it came forth through birth. But in this circuit the faithful soul keeps a solemn day, because, while through inward joy it is fixed in the love of things eternal, it keeps holiday from every unlawful occupation without. But when the soul calls back to memory the evils 320Bdone before, and from the consideration of them is the more kindled through compunction, they are, as it were, invited from the circuit, as men about to celebrate, to the solemnity. As it is said by the Psalmist: The remainders of thought shall keep a festival day to thee (Psal. LXXV). But because before the ardor of compunction the remembrance of past evils shakes the mind of the sinner with a certain terror of its own guilt, rightly does he say that these are called from the circuit both to the solemnity alike and to terror. Yet it is to be known that he is terrified in one way who remembers that he has done no good thing, and in another way he who knows that with his good acts certain evils are mingled for which he should fear. When therefore a man looks around upon his own actions, and finds in them certain things wherein he may be confident, but certain things for which he should fear, he is indeed terrified, 320Cbut not from the circuit; because on the altar the sinful conscience is straitened by the memory of its own guilt, while on the other side it is enlarged in the confidence of good work. But when on every side his own deeds accuse the sinner's soul, then he is terrified as it were in a circuit; because seeing on this side and on that all things to be dreaded, he is hemmed in within himself by anguish alone. This also is fittingly said in the person of the sinful soul, when, going forth out of the body, it is troubled by the memory of its evils meeting it on every side. Whence also it is rightly said: Thou hast called. For a man, so long as he is in this life, ought assiduously to call back to memory the evils done before, that he may of his own accord terrify himself unto repentance, as the Psalmist testifies of himself, saying: 320DMy sin is before [contra] me always (Psal. L). And in another place the same man says: I will think for my sin (Psal. XXXVII). But whoever here has been unwilling to remember his own evils, there God will call them before the eyes of his mind in testimony of his damnation, so that from the memory of them he may be terrified there unto punishment who here was unwilling to be terrified wholesomely unto repentance. As the divine voice speaks by the same Psalmist, saying: I will reprove thee, and set myself against thy face (Psal. XLIX). And the manner of this calling is further shown when it is said: As to a solemn day. For a solemn day is a day of quiet, a day of calling, when it is not lawful to work, but each man then begins to enjoy with gladness those things which he had before prepared for himself with labor. What then is the day of death but 321Aa solemn day, on which there no longer remains any power of working, but each man begins to enjoy the wages and reward of his works and of his past labors? There follows:

And there was none in the day of the fury of the Lord that escaped and was left. The day of the fury of the Lord is the end of every man: when each is compelled with great strictness to render an account, who before, in his life, was kindly awaited unto repentance as it were in a day of mercy. Rightly therefore is it said of the day of fury: There was none that escaped and was left. That escaped, namely, his conscience; that was left unto repentance. Note what is said: That escaped, namely, his conscience. A bad conscience is a great torment. But sinners can here escape their 321Bconscience, while, given over to outward delights, they meanwhile in a manner hand over to forgetfulness the evils which they endure within. For the thorn of conscience now pricks the heart less when the swaddling-band (if I may so speak) of earthly delight covers it, wrapped about. But there they could not escape the torment of conscience: because when the sense of the flesh is shut without in death, the horror of past evils is laid open within to the soul. Then the unhappy soul, though pricked with compunction too late, would now amend by repentance what it cannot escape by conscience. But by the just judgment of God, since it neglected the times formerly granted to it as a penitent, it is now not left; because once the irrevocable sentence has gone forth, it is no longer deferred in this life unto repentance. Rightly therefore is it said. 322AThere was none in the day of the fury of the Lord that escaped and was left. Because when the hour of death has come upon him, then each is compelled to return to his own conscience, that with it accompanying him he may be carried over from this life without delay to receive the sentence of the judge. There follows: Whom I brought up and nourished, the enemy hath consumed them. Lest thou think that the punishment of conscience alone is prepared for sinners: whom I brought up, he says, and nourished, the enemy hath consumed them: because assuredly the strength and beauty of mortal bodies, which here is fed by unlawful desires, shall there be consumed in torments by the angels that torture. And it is said to be consumed, not because the punishment reduces the substance to nothing, but because the pain, running through even to the inmost parts of nature, leaves nothing untouched. Or by those brought up 322Band nourished we may understand the very desires belonging to the flesh: which are brought up by pride and nourished by luxury; and the more we feed them by fulfilling them, the more we inflame them to appetite. These, brought up and nourished, the enemy consumes — that is, death, of which the Apostle says: Last of all the enemy death shall be destroyed (I Cor. XV): because assuredly, when our flesh is cut down in death, all its desires are extinguished together, as it is said by the Psalmist: In that day all their thoughts shall perish (Psal. XLV). And consider how fittingly at the end of the lament the last punishment of the sinful soul is bewailed: that we may understand how great a misery follows upon those evils which have been foretold.
